Organic Chemistry Principles — Classification, IUPAC Nomenclature, Isomerism & Mechanisms

Organic chemistry is the study of carbon compounds. Carbon forms four covalent bonds (tetravalency) and catenates extensively — bonding with itself to form chains, branches, and rings. Organic compounds are classified by functional groups — specific atoms or groups that determine chemical reactivity. Hydrocarbons contain only C and H (alkanes C₂Hₖ₊₂, alkenes C₂Hₖ, alkynes C₂Hₖ−₂, arenes). Functional groups include: halogens (R-X), alcohols (R-OH), ethers (R-O-R'), aldehydes (R-CHO), ketones (R-CO-R'), carboxylic acids (R-COOH), esters (R-COOR'), amines (R-NH₂), amides (R-CONH₂), and nitro compounds (R-NO₂). IUPAC nomenclature systematically names compounds: select the longest carbon chain (parent), number to give substituents the lowest locants, name substituents alphabetically, and indicate stereochemistry (E/Z, R/S). Isomerism: structural isomers (same molecular formula, different connectivity) and stereoisomers (same connectivity, different spatial arrangement — geometrical cis/trans and optical enantiomers). Reaction mechanisms describe bond breaking and forming: homolytic cleavage (radicals) and heterolytic cleavage (carbocations/carbanions). Major types: substitution (SN1, SN2), elimination (E1, E2), addition (electrophilic, nucleophilic), and rearrangement.

"Organic chemistry is the chemistry of carbon compounds. Biochemistry is the study of carbon compounds that crawl."
— Adapted from Mike Adams
Part 1

📖 Classification and IUPAC Nomenclature

Fundamentals
Classification by Functional Groups, IUPAC Rules, Common Nomenclature, and Trivial Names
Organic compounds are broadly classified as acyclic (open-chain) or cyclic (closed-ring). Cyclic compounds include alicyclic (saturated rings), aromatic (planar rings with conjugated π-electron systems following Hückel's rule: 4n+2 π electrons), and heterocyclic (ring contains O, N, S). Functional groups determine chemical properties: alkenes (C=C), alkynes (C≡C), arenes (Ar-H), alkyl halides (R-X), alcohols (R-OH), phenols (Ar-OH), ethers (R-O-R'), aldehydes (R-CHO), ketones (R-CO-R'), carboxylic acids (R-COOH), esters (R-COOR'), acyl halides (R-COX), amides (R-CONH₂), amines (R-NH₂), nitriles (R-C≡N), and nitro compounds (R-NO₂). IUPAC nomenclature (IUPAC 1979/1993): (1) identify the parent chain — the longest continuous carbon chain containing the principal functional group. (2) Number the chain to give the principal functional group the lowest possible locant (OH > CHO > CO > COOH > COOR > CONH₂ > CN > C=C > C≡C > R > X > NO₂). (3) Name substituents as prefixes in alphabetical order (ignoring di-, tri-). (4) Use suffixes to indicate the principal functional group (-ol, -al, -one, -oic acid, -oate, -amide, -nitrile, -amine). (5) For alkenes/alkynes, use -ene/-yne; indicate position with locants. (6) Stereochemistry: E/Z for alkenes, R/S for chiral centers. Common names: formaldehyde (methanal), acetic acid (ethanoic acid), acetone (propanone).
Systematic IUPAC naming gives each organic compound a unique, unambiguous name based on parent chain, functional group priority, and substituent positions.
Part 2

🔀 Structural and Stereoisomerism

Isomerism
Chain, Position, Functional Group, Metamerism, Tautomerism, Geometrical and Optical Isomerism
Structural isomers have the same molecular formula but different bond connectivity. Types: chain isomerism (different carbon skeletons — e.g., n-butane vs isobutane), position isomerism (same functional group at different positions — e.g., 1-propanol vs 2-propanol), functional group isomerism (different functional groups — e.g., ethanol CH₃CH₂OH vs dimethyl ether CH₃OCH₃), metamerism (different alkyl groups around a functional group — e.g., diethyl ether vs methyl propyl ether), and tautomerism (rapid interconversion between isomers via proton transfer — e.g., keto-enol tautomerism in carbonyl compounds). Stereoisomers have the same bond connectivity but different spatial arrangement. Geometrical isomerism (cis-trans): arises from restricted rotation around a double bond or in cyclic compounds. For alkenes, cis (same side) and trans (opposite side); E/Z notation based on Cahn-Ingold-Prelog priority rules. Optical isomerism: molecules that are non-superimposable on their mirror images are chiral. A carbon with four different substituents is a chiral center (stereocenter). Enantiomers are non-superimposable mirror images — they rotate plane-polarized light in opposite directions (dextrorotatory +, levorotatory −). A 1:1 mixture of enantiomers is a racemic mixture (racemate), which is optically inactive. Diastereomers are stereoisomers that are not mirror images (e.g., cis/trans isomers, or compounds with multiple chiral centers). Meso compounds have multiple chiral centers but are achiral due to an internal plane of symmetry.
Structural isomers differ in connectivity; stereoisomers differ in spatial arrangement — the latter includes cis/trans and chiral enantiomers crucial in biological systems.
Part 3

🔥 Reaction Mechanisms: Substitution and Elimination

Mechanisms
SN1, SN2, E1, E2 Reactions — Kinetics, Stereochemistry, and Factors Affecting Reactivity
SN2 (bimolecular nucleophilic substitution): one-step concerted mechanism. The nucleophile attacks from the back side, and the leaving group departs simultaneously. Inversion of configuration (Walden inversion). Kinetics: rate = k[RX][Nu⁻] — second order. Favored by: primary substrates, strong nucleophiles, polar aprotic solvents (DMSO, DMF, acetone), good leaving groups (I⁻ > Br⁻ > Cl⁻ > F⁻). SN1 (unimolecular nucleophilic substitution): two-step mechanism. Step 1: slow ionization to form a carbocation (rate-determining). Step 2: fast attack by nucleophile. Racemization occurs (partial inversion + partial retention). Kinetics: rate = k[RX] — first order. Favored by: tertiary substrates, weak nucleophiles, polar protic solvents (H₂O, ROH), good leaving groups. Carbocation stability: 3° > 2° > 1° > methyl. E2 (bimolecular elimination): one-step concerted mechanism — base abstracts a β-hydrogen while the leaving group departs, forming an alkene. Anti-periplanar geometry required (H and leaving group must be 180° apart). Kinetics: rate = k[RX][base] — second order. Favored by: strong bulky bases (t-BuO⁻), high temperature, aprotic solvents. Follows Zaitsev's rule: the more substituted alkene (more stable) is the major product. E1 (unimolecular elimination): two-step mechanism — ionization to carbocation (slow), then deprotonation (fast). Kinetics: rate = k[RX] — first order. Favored by: tertiary substrates, weak bases, polar protic solvents, high temperature. Carbocation rearrangements may occur (hydride or alkyl shift) to form a more stable carbocation. Substitution vs elimination competition: strong base/nucleophile + primary = SN2; bulky base + tertiary = E2; weak nucleophile + tertiary = SN1/E1 mixture; high temperature favors elimination.
SN2 inverts stereochemistry with second-order kinetics; SN1 gives racemization with first-order kinetics; E2 requires anti-periplanar geometry; Zaitsev's rule predicts the major alkene product.
Part 4

⚡ Addition Reactions, Rearrangements, and Functional Group Transformations

Transformations
Electrophilic Addition to Alkenes, Markovnikov's Rule, Carbocation Rearrangements, and Oxidation-Reduction
Electrophilic addition to alkenes: the C=C π-bond is electron-rich and attracts electrophiles. General mechanism: electrophile attacks, forming a carbocation, then nucleophile attacks. Markovnikov's rule: in HX addition, the H adds to the less substituted carbon (the one with more H's), giving the more stable carbocation intermediate. Anti-Markovnikov addition occurs with HBr in the presence of peroxides (radical mechanism — Kharasch effect). Addition of halogens (X₂): anti addition via cyclic halonium ion intermediate — gives vicinal dihalides (e.g., Br₂ in CCl₄). Addition of H₂O (acid-catalyzed hydration): follows Markovnikov, gives alcohols. Hydroboration-oxidation: anti-Markovnikov addition of H₂O (BH₃ then H₂O₂/OH⁻) — syn addition, gives alcohols. Oxidation of alcohols: primary → aldehyde → carboxylic acid (using PCC or CrO₃/H₂SO₄); secondary → ketone; tertiary — no oxidation. Reduction: aldehydes/ketones → alcohols (NaBH₄, LiAlH₄); carboxylic acids → alcohols (LiAlH₄); esters → alcohols (LiAlH₄); nitriles → amines (H₂/Ni). Carbocation rearrangements: 1,2-hydride shift or 1,2-alkyl shift to form a more stable carbocation (e.g., in dehydration of alcohols or addition of HX). Electrophilic aromatic substitution (EAS): nitration, halogenation, sulfonation, Friedel-Crafts alkylation/acylation. The aromatic ring acts as a nucleophile; the π-complex (σ-complex, Wheland intermediate) is the key intermediate. Directing effects: activating (—OH, —NH₂, —R) are ortho/para directing; deactivating (—NO₂, —CN, —CHO, —COOH) are meta directing; halogens are deactivating but ortho/para directing.
Electrophilic addition follows Markovnikov's rule via stable carbocation intermediates; oxidation, reduction, and EAS reactions enable systematic functional group interconversion in organic synthesis.

🎯 Practice

← Back to Chemistry

Pericyclic Reactions and the Woodward–Hoffmann Rules

Pericyclic reactions proceed through a cyclic, concerted transition state in which bonding changes occur simultaneously without discrete ionic or radical intermediates. The three principal classes are cycloadditions, electrocyclic reactions, and sigmatropic rearrangements. The Woodward–Hoffmann rules predict the allowedness of each process by correlating the symmetry of the interacting molecular orbitals with the reaction conditions (thermal vs. photochemical). For a cycloaddition, the suprafacial–suprafacial [4+2] Diels–Alder reaction is thermally allowed because the HOMO of the diene (four π electrons) and the LUMO of the dienophile (two π electrons) have matching symmetry; the reverse [2+2] cycloaddition is thermally forbidden but becomes allowed under photochemical excitation where one component is promoted to an excited state. Electrocyclic ring closures of conjugated polyenes follow a conrotatory pathway for 4n π‑electron systems under thermal conditions and a disrotatory pathway for 4n+2 systems; photochemical conditions invert these preferences. Sigmatropic shifts such as the [1,5] hydrogen shift in pentadienyl systems are thermally allowed suprafacially because the migrating σ‑bond interacts with a six‑electron π‑system that satisfies the Hückel aromatic transition‑state criterion. The rules are derived from the conservation of orbital symmetry and can be applied using the correlation diagram or the simpler “aromatic transition state” method: a pericyclic reaction is thermally allowed when the cyclic array of interacting orbitals contains (4n+2) electrons (Hückel aromatic) and photochemically allowed when it contains 4n electrons (Möbius aromatic). Mastery of these principles enables rapid prediction of stereochemical outcomes — for example, the thermal ring closure of (2E,4Z,6E)-octatriene proceeds conrotatorily to give a trans‑substituted cyclohexadiene, whereas the photochemical closure gives the cis isomer.

Organometallic Reagents in Carbon–Carbon Bond Formation

Organometallic reagents provide nucleophilic carbon centers that attack electrophilic carbonyl carbons, alkyl halides, or other π‑systems, forming new C–C bonds. Grignard reagents (RMgX) and organolithium compounds (RLi) are strong bases and nucleophiles; they add to aldehydes, ketones, esters, and carbon dioxide to give alcohols after work‑up, but they also deprotonate acidic protons (water, alcohols, terminal alkynes) and undergo β‑hydride elimination with certain alkyl halides, limiting their use with substrates bearing β‑hydrogens. Gilman reagents (lithium dialkylcuprates, R₂CuLi) are softer nucleophiles that effect conjugate (1,4) addition to α,β‑unsaturated carbonyl compounds and undergo SN2‑type coupling with primary alkyl halides and vinyl/aryl halides without β‑hydride elimination. The Suzuki–Miyaura coupling employs an aryl or vinyl boronic acid (ArB(OH)₂) with an aryl/vinyl halide (Ar′X) in the presence of a palladium(0) catalyst (e.g., Pd(PPh₃)₄) and a base (Na₂CO₃, K₃PO₄) to forge biaryl bonds under mild, aqueous‑compatible conditions. The Heck reaction couples an aryl/vinyl halide with an alkene using a Pd(0) catalyst and a base (Et₃N) to give substituted alkenes with retention of alkene geometry. The Stille coupling uses organostannanes (R–SnBu₃) under similar palladium catalysis, while the Negishi coupling employs organozinc halides (R–ZnX). Each method tolerates different functional groups: Suzuki tolerates esters, ketones, and nitriles; Stille tolerates aldehydes but requires removal of toxic tin byproducts; Negishi offers high chemoselectivity for sensitive groups. Catalyst choice (Pd(PPh₃)₄, Pd(dppf)Cl₂, Pd₂(dba)₃ with phosphine ligands) and ligand electronics control oxidative addition, transmetalation, and reductive elimination rates, influencing yields and stereoretention. Understanding the mechanistic steps — oxidative addition of the aryl halide to Pd(0), transmetalation with the organometallic partner, and reductive elimination to form the C–C bond — allows rational optimization of cross‑coupling protocols for complex molecule synthesis.

Mechanistic Analysis of a Suzuki Coupling
Predict the major product and explain the stereochemical outcome when (E)-β-bromostyrene is coupled with phenylboronic acid under standard Suzuki conditions (Pd(PPh₃)₄, Na₂CO₃, aqueous ethanol, 80 °C).
The oxidative addition of (E)-β-bromostyrene to Pd(0) occurs with retention of the alkene geometry, giving a vinyl‑palladium(II) intermediate where the palladium is bound to the β‑carbon. Transmetalation with phenylboronic acid transfers the phenyl group to palladium, forming a diaryl‑vinyl‑palladium(II) species. Reductive elimination couples the phenyl group to the α‑carbon of the original styrene fragment, producing (E)-stilbene (Ph–CH=CH–Ph). Because each step proceeds with retention at the vinyl carbon, the (E) configuration of the starting alkene is preserved in the product. No isomerization occurs under the mild basic conditions, so the major product is (E)-stilbene.
Exam‑Day Strategy for Cross‑Coupling Questions
When a question asks to choose the appropriate coupling method, first identify the hybridization of the electrophilic carbon (sp² vs. sp³) and the presence of β‑hydrogens. For sp²–sp² biaryl formation, Suzuki is preferred for its functional‑group tolerance and low toxicity. For sp²–sp³ couplings where the alkyl partner has β‑hydrogens, Negishi or Kumada (with Ni) often outperform Suzuki because transmetalation is faster and β‑hydride elimination is suppressed. Memorize the typical catalyst/ligand combinations: Pd(PPh₃)₄ for simple Suzuki, Pd(dppf)Cl₂ for hindered aryl chlorides, and NiCl₂(dppp) for Kumada. A quick mental checklist — electrophile type, nucleophile type, functional groups, toxicity constraints — narrows the answer to one or two viable methods.

Asymmetric Synthesis: Chiral Catalysts and Auxiliaries

Asymmetric synthesis aims to create a single enantiomer or diastereomer preferentially. Chiral catalysis uses a sub‑stoichiometric amount of a chiral ligand bound to a metal center to induce enantioselectivity in a reaction that would otherwise give a racemic mixture. The Sharpless asymmetric epoxidation employs a titanium(IV) isopropoxide complex with a chiral tartrate ester (D‑ or L‑diethyl tartrate) to epoxidize allylic alcohols with >90 % ee; the allylic alcohol coordinates to titanium, fixing the substrate orientation and delivering the peroxide to one face of the double bond. The Noyori asymmetric hydrogenation uses a ruthenium(II) complex bearing a chiral diphosphine (BINAP) and a chiral diamine (DPEN) to reduce β‑keto esters and enamides with excellent enantiocontrol; the chiral pocket directs hydride delivery to the pro‑R or pro‑S face of the carbonyl. Organocatalysis avoids metals: proline and its derivatives catalyze aldol reactions via an enamine intermediate, giving anti‑aldol products with high enantioselectivity; MacMillan imidazolidinone catalysts enable asymmetric Diels–Alder and Michael additions through iminium activation. Chiral auxiliaries are stoichiometric, covalently attached groups that direct the stereochemical course of a reaction and are removed afterward. The Evans oxazolidinone auxiliary, derived from (S)- or (R)-phenylglycinol, controls the facial selectivity of enolate alkylations, aldol reactions, and Michael additions; after the key C–C bond formation, the auxiliary is cleaved by LiBH₄ reduction or transesterification, delivering the chiral product in high ee. The Oppolzer camphorsultam auxiliary operates similarly for enolate alkylations and cycloadditions. The choice between catalytic and auxiliary approaches depends on substrate availability, cost, and the need for scalability; catalytic methods are atom‑economic but may require extensive ligand screening, whereas auxiliaries give predictable, high selectivity at the expense of extra steps for attachment and removal.

Spectroscopic Methods for Structural Elucidation

Modern organic structure determination relies on a combination of infrared (IR) spectroscopy, nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR) spectroscopy, and mass spectrometry (MS). IR absorption bands identify functional groups: a broad O–H stretch (3200–3600 cm⁻¹) for alcohols and carboxylic acids, a sharp C=O stretch near 1700 cm⁻¹ (ketones ~1715, aldehydes ~1725, esters ~1735, amides ~1650 cm⁻¹), and C≡C/C≡N stretches around 2100–2260 cm⁻¹. ¹H NMR provides the number of distinct proton environments (chemical shift δ), integration ratios, and coupling patterns (singlet, doublet, triplet, multiplet) governed by the n+1 rule for vicinal couplings (³J_HH ≈ 7–8 Hz for typical alkyl chains). Diastereotopic protons on a CH₂ adjacent to a stereocenter appear as separate signals (AB system) with geminal coupling (²J ≈ 12–14 Hz). ¹³C NMR (broadband decoupled) reveals the number of unique carbon atoms; DEPT experiments differentiate CH₃, CH₂, CH, and quaternary carbons. Two‑dimensional experiments — COSY (¹H–¹H correlation), HSQC (¹H–¹³C single‑bond), HMBC (¹H–¹³C long‑range, 2–3 bonds) — map connectivity and establish carbon skeletons. NOESY/ROESY gives through‑space proximities, enabling relative stereochemistry assignment. High‑resolution mass spectrometry (HRMS) provides exact molecular formula (≤5 ppm error) by measuring the monoisotopic mass; isotopic patterns (M+1, M+2) confirm the presence of chlorine, bromine, or sulfur. Fragmentation patterns in electron‑impact (EI) MS reveal structural motifs: α‑cleavage next to heteroatoms, McLafferty rearrangement for carbonyl compounds, and loss of 15 Da (CH₃) or 18 Da (H₂O). Combining these data — molecular formula from HRMS, functional groups from IR, connectivity from ¹H/¹³C NMR and 2D correlations, and stereochemistry from NOE — yields a complete, unambiguous structure for unknown natural products or synthetic intermediates.

Polymerization Mechanisms: Step‑Growth and Chain‑Growth

Polymerization is classified by the kinetic mechanism of chain propagation. Step‑growth (condensation) polymerization occurs when any two molecular species — monomers, oligomers, or polymers — can react, and the molecular weight increases gradually; the degree of polymerization (DPₙ) is related to the extent of reaction p by DPₙ = 1/(1−p). Typical examples are polyesterification (diacid + diol), polyamidation (diamine + diacid chloride), and polyurethane formation (diisocyanate + diol). Each step eliminates a small molecule (water, HCl, alcohol). High molecular weight requires p > 0.99, demanding stoichiometric balance and removal of condensate. Chain‑growth (addition) polymerization proceeds via an active center (radical, cation, anion, or metal‑complex) that adds monomer units rapidly; the polymer chain grows only at the active site. Free‑radical polymerization of vinyl monomers (styrene, methyl methacrylate) uses initiators (AIBN, benzoyl peroxide) that decompose to radicals; propagation rate constant k_p and termination (combination or disproportionation) determine molecular weight distribution (Đ = M_w/M_n ≈ 1.5–2 for termination by combination). Cationic polymerization (isobutylene, vinyl ethers) requires strong Lewis acids (BF₃·OEt₂) and low temperatures to suppress chain transfer. Anionic polymerization (styrene, butadiene) with organolithium initiators yields living polymers — no termination, narrow Đ ≈ 1.01 — enabling block copolymers by sequential monomer addition. Coordination polymerization (Ziegler–Natta, metallocene) uses transition‑metal catalysts (TiCl₄/AlR₃, Cp₂ZrCl₂/MAO) to polymerize α‑olefins with controlled tacticity (isotactic, syndiotactic). The choice of mechanism dictates polymer architecture, molecular weight control, and end‑group functionality, which in turn govern material properties such as crystallinity, tensile strength, and thermal behavior.

  • Assuming all pericyclic reactions follow the same stereochemical rule — thermal vs. photochemical conditions invert the allowed pathway for each class.
  • Treating Grignard reagents as universally compatible with any electrophile — they are destroyed by protic functional groups and undergo side reactions with β‑hydrogen‑bearing alkyl halides.
  • Confusing the stereochemical outcome of Suzuki coupling — the geometry of the vinyl halide is retained, not inverted, through the catalytic cycle.
  • Believing chiral auxiliaries are catalytic — they are used in stoichiometric amounts and must be attached and removed.
  • Neglecting the need for exact mass in HRMS — low‑resolution MS cannot distinguish isobaric formulas (e.g., C₈H₈O₂ vs. C₇H₁₂N₂).
  • Assuming step‑growth polymers reach high molecular weight early — DPₙ only becomes large near quantitative conversion (p > 0.99).
  • Overlooking that living anionic polymerization requires rigorously anhydrous, oxygen‑free conditions; trace impurities terminate the active chain ends.

These advanced topics extend the core curriculum and frequently appear in higher‑level problem sets and research‑oriented examinations.

Photochemical Reactions: Norrish Type I and Type II Processes

Photochemical activation of carbonyl compounds proceeds through the n→π* excited state, which can undergo two fundamentally different cleavage pathways. In a Norrish Type I reaction the α‑C–C bond adjacent to the carbonyl group breaks homolytically, generating an acyl radical and an alkyl radical. The acyl radical rapidly loses carbon monoxide to give a second alkyl radical; both radicals may recombine, abstract hydrogen, or undergo β‑scission. The reaction is favored for ketones that can form relatively stable radicals, such as α‑aryl or α‑tert‑butyl ketones, and it competes efficiently with intersystem crossing to the triplet state. Quantum yields for Type I cleavage are typically low (Φ ≈ 0.01–0.1) because the excited state also decays by fluorescence and non‑radiative pathways.

In contrast, a Norrish Type II reaction involves intramolecular hydrogen abstraction from the γ‑carbon (four atoms away from the carbonyl oxygen) by the excited carbonyl oxygen, forming a 1,4‑biradical (or a cyclic oxetane‑like transition state). The biradical can undergo two distinct fragmentations: (i) cleavage of the α‑β bond to give an enol (which tautomerises to a carbonyl) and an alkene, or (ii) cyclisation to a cyclobutanol. The hydrogen‑abstraction step requires a six‑membered cyclic transition state; therefore, the γ‑hydrogen must be suitably aligned (typically a gauche conformation). Substituents that stabilise the resulting radicals — such as allylic or benzylic positions — accelerate the reaction. The overall quantum yield for Type II processes can be higher (Φ ≈ 0.1–0.5) when the conformational requirement is met.

Both pathways are sensitive to solvent polarity and temperature. Polar solvents stabilise the charge‑separated character of the n→π* state and can increase the rate of intersystem crossing, often favouring Type I cleavage. Low temperatures suppress conformational mobility, reducing the population of the required gauche geometry for Type II abstraction. Photolysis of cyclic ketones (e.g., cyclopentanone) illustrates the competition: the rigid ring enforces a favorable γ‑hydrogen geometry, so Type II dominates, giving ring‑opened products. In acyclic ketones, conformational freedom allows both pathways, and product ratios become diagnostic of the excited‑state lifetime and the relative stability of the radical intermediates.

Experimental detection of the biradical intermediate in Type II reactions has been achieved by time‑resolved electron paramagnetic resonance (EPR) and by trapping with radical scavengers such as TEMPO. The presence of a cyclobutanol side product is a hallmark of the cyclisation channel, while the formation of an alkene and a carbonyl fragment signals the cleavage channel. Understanding the balance between these channels enables synthetic chemists to design photochemical steps that selectively generate either alkenes (useful for olefination) or cyclobutanol scaffolds (precursors to cyclobutane rings).

Heterocyclic Chemistry: Aromaticity and Reactivity of Five‑Membered Rings

Five‑membered heteroaromatics — pyrrole, furan, thiophene, and their benzo‑fused analogues indole, benzofuran, benzothiophene — possess 6 π‑electrons delocalised over the ring, satisfying Hückel’s rule. The heteroatom contributes two electrons from a lone pair into the π‑system, while the remaining four carbon atoms each contribute one p‑electron. This electron‑rich character makes them markedly more reactive toward electrophilic aromatic substitution (EAS) than benzene. The order of reactivity is pyrrole > furan > thiophene > benzene, reflecting the decreasing ability of the heteroatom to donate electron density (N > O > S).

In pyrrole the nitrogen lone pair is fully conjugated; protonation occurs at carbon (C‑2) rather than nitrogen because N‑protonation destroys aromaticity. Consequently, pyrrole is a very weak base (pKₐ of conjugate acid ≈ −0.4) but a strong nucleophile at C‑2 and C‑3. Electrophilic substitution proceeds preferentially at C‑2; if C‑2 is blocked, C‑3 reacts. Furan behaves similarly but the oxygen lone pair is less effectively donated, so furan is less reactive and more prone to side reactions such as Diels–Alder cycloaddition across the 2,3‑positions. Thiophene’s sulfur atom uses a 3p orbital for conjugation, giving a more stable aromatic system; it undergoes EAS at C‑2 under milder conditions than benzene but is less reactive than furan.

The heteroatom also directs metal‑halogen exchange and lithiation. Directed ortho‑metalation (DoM) of 2‑substituted thiophenes with n‑BuLi occurs at C‑5, whereas pyrrole and furan are typically metalated at C‑2 after N‑ or O‑protection (e.g., N‑TIPS, O‑Me). The resulting organometallic intermediates are valuable for cross‑coupling (Suzuki, Stille, Negishi) to build complex heteroaryl architectures.

Oxidative polymerisation of pyrrole, thiophene, and their derivatives yields conducting polymers (polypyrrole, polythiophene). The mechanism involves radical cation formation at the 2‑position, followed by coupling and rearomatization. Substituents at the 3‑position (e.g., alkyl chains) improve solubility and processability without disrupting conjugation. In contrast, furan polymerises less readily because the resulting polyfuran is unstable to oxidation.

Benzo‑fusion (indole, benzofuran, benzothiophene) extends the π‑system, lowering the HOMO‑LUMO gap and shifting absorption to longer wavelengths. Indole’s nitrogen is part of a fused benzene ring; its reactivity at C‑3 dominates (the “pyrrole‑like” position), while C‑2 is less activated. This regioselectivity underpins the biosynthesis of tryptophan and the synthesis of many alkaloids. Benzofuran and benzothiophene show analogous C‑2/ C‑3 reactivity patterns but with reduced electron density due to the benzene ring’s inductive withdrawal.

Worked Example: Norrish Type II Cleavage
Predict the major products when 2‑octanone (CH₃CO(CH₂)₅CH₃) is irradiated in hexane at 25 °C. Indicate the mechanistic pathway and the structures of the fragments.
The carbonyl n→π* triplet abstracts the γ‑hydrogen (the hydrogen on C‑5) via a six‑membered cyclic transition state, forming a 1,4‑biradical. β‑Scission of the α‑β bond (C‑2–C‑3) yields an enol that tautomerises to butanal (CH₃CH₂CH₂CHO) and a terminal alkene, 1‑hexene (CH₂=CH(CH₂)₃CH₃). The cyclisation channel to a cyclobutanol is disfavoured because the resulting four‑membered ring would be highly strained in an acyclic system. Thus the major products are butanal and 1‑hexene.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a photochemistry question appears, first identify the carbonyl substrate and count the carbon atoms to locate the γ‑hydrogen. Draw the six‑membered transition state; if the geometry is feasible, write the Type II pathway. If the substrate lacks a γ‑hydrogen or the conformation is locked unfavourably, default to Type I cleavage. Remember that quantum yields are rarely asked numerically; focus on product prediction and mechanistic arrows.

Retrosynthetic Analysis: The Disconnection Approach and Synthons

Retrosynthetic analysis, formalised by E. J. Corey, deconstructs a target molecule into simpler precursors by imaginary bond disconnections. Each disconnection generates a pair of synthons — idealised reactive fragments — which are then matched to commercially available or easily prepared reagents (synthetic equivalents). The power of the method lies in recognizing strategic bonds whose cleavage simplifies the carbon skeleton, reduces functional‑group complexity, or reveals symmetry.

A classic example is the disconnection of a β‑keto ester (e.g., ethyl acetoacetate) at the C–C bond α to the carbonyl, yielding an acyl anion synthon (CH₃CO⁻) and an ester carbonyl cation synthon (⁺CH₂CO₂Et). The synthetic equivalents are a lithium enolate of acetone (or a silyl enol ether) and ethyl chloroformate. This logic underpins the Claisen condensation and its variants. For more complex targets, the “functional‑group interconversion” (FGI) step is used to transform a synthon into a more accessible equivalent, such as converting a carboxylic acid synthon into a nitrile (via dehydration) or a Weinreb amide (for controlled addition of organometallics).

Strategic disconnections often exploit latent polarity: a C–C bond formed by a nucleophilic addition to a carbonyl can be disconnected to give a nucleophilic carbon synthon (carbanion) and an electrophilic carbonyl synthon. The “umpolung” concept (polarity inversion) expands the toolbox; a dithiane anion serves as an acyl anion equivalent, allowing the formation of C–C bonds at positions that are normally electrophilic. Similarly, the use of a cyanide anion as a formyl anion equivalent enables the introduction of a carbonyl group after hydrolysis.

Ring‑forming disconnections are guided by the “ring‑opening” principle: a cyclic target can be opened at a bond that would be formed in the forward synthesis by an intramolecular cyclisation (e.g., Dieckmann condensation, intramolecular Michael addition, or ring‑closing metathesis). The choice of disconnection must consider the ring size, substitution pattern, and the feasibility of the cyclisation under thermodynamic or kinetic control. For macrocycles, high‑dilution conditions or template‑directed strategies are anticipated in the forward direction.

Modern retrosynthetic software (e.g., SYNTHIA, RetroPath) encodes these principles as graph‑search algorithms, but human expertise remains essential for evaluating protecting‑group strategies, stereochemical outcomes, and step economy. A well‑designed retrosynthetic tree typically contains 5–10 disconnections for a molecule of 20–30 carbons, each step justified by a known reliable reaction. The final synthetic plan is then linearised, optimised for convergent assembly, and subjected to a “forward‑check” to confirm reagent compatibility and overall yield.

Green Chemistry Metrics: Atom Economy, E‑Factor, and Process Mass Intensity

Green chemistry quantifies the environmental impact of a synthetic route through a set of mass‑based metrics. Atom economy (AE), introduced by Barry Trost, is defined as the molecular weight of the desired product divided by the sum of molecular weights of all reactants, expressed as a percentage. A reaction with 100 % AE (e.g., a Diels–Alder cycloaddition) incorporates every atom of the starting materials into the product, generating no stoichiometric by‑products. In contrast, a Wittig reaction has an AE of roughly 50 % because the phosphine oxide by‑product accounts for half the mass of the reagents.

The E‑factor (environmental factor) measures the mass of waste generated per mass of product isolated: E = (total mass of inputs − mass of product) / mass of product. It includes solvents, reagents, work‑up materials, and any unrecovered catalysts. Typical fine‑chemical processes have E‑factors of 5–50, whereas bulk commodity chemicals aim for E < 1. Reducing the E‑factor is achieved by solvent recycling, using catalytic rather than stoichiometric reagents, and designing telescoped sequences that avoid intermediate isolation.

Process mass intensity (PMI) is a broader metric: PMI = total mass of all materials entering the process (including water, solvents, and reagents) divided by mass of product. PMI = E + 1. It captures the full material throughput and is increasingly used in life‑cycle assessments. A PMI of 10 means ten kilograms of material are processed for each kilogram of product. Process optimisation targets PMI < 5 for high‑value pharmaceuticals.

These metrics are complementary. A reaction may have excellent atom economy but a high E‑factor if large volumes of solvent are used and not recovered. Conversely, a low‑AE reaction (e.g., a substitution that generates inorganic salt) can achieve a modest E‑factor if the by‑product is benign and the solvent is recycled. The “green chemistry hierarchy” prioritises: (1) prevention of waste, (2) atom‑economical transformations, (3) use of renewable feedstocks, (4) catalytic processes, (5) design for degradation. When evaluating a synthetic route for an exam, calculate AE for each step, sum the E‑factors (or PMI) for the overall sequence, and identify the step that contributes most to waste — often a protection/deprotection or a stoichiometric oxidation.

Practical illustration: the industrial synthesis of ibuprofen via the Boots route (six steps, AE ≈ 40 %, E‑factor ≈ 30) was replaced by the Hoechst three‑step catalytic carbonylation route (AE ≈ 80 %, E‑factor ≈ 5). The newer route eliminates a stoichiometric Friedel‑Crafts acylation and a reduction, replacing them with a palladium‑catalysed carbonylation that incorporates CO directly into the product. This case study demonstrates how retrosynthetic redesign aligned with green metrics can dramatically improve sustainability.

Organocatalysis: Enamine, Iminium, and Hydrogen‑Bond Activation

Organocatalysis employs small organic molecules — typically secondary amines, phosphoric acids, or thioureas — to activate substrates through covalent or non‑covalent interactions, avoiding transition metals. The two dominant covalent activation modes are enamine catalysis and iminium catalysis, both mediated by a chiral secondary amine (often derived from proline). In enamine catalysis, the amine condenses with an aldehyde or ketone to form an enamine, raising the HOMO of the carbonyl α‑carbon and enabling nucleophilic attack on electrophiles such as α,β‑unsaturated carbonyls (Michael addition), alkyl halides (α‑alkylation), or diazo compounds (C–C bond formation). The chiral environment of the catalyst controls the facial selectivity, delivering high enantiomeric excess (often > 95 % ee).

Iminium catalysis proceeds by condensation of the same amine with an α,β‑unsaturated aldehyde or ketone, generating an iminium ion that lowers the LUMO of the β‑carbon. This activates the Michael acceptor toward nucleophilic attack by enolates, silyl enol ethers, or indoles. The iminium geometry (E/Z) and the catalyst’s steric bulk dictate the approach trajectory, again providing excellent stereocontrol. A hallmark of iminium catalysis is the ability to perform cascade reactions: an initial Michael addition creates a new enolate that can undergo a second intramolecular cyclisation before catalyst release.

Non‑covalent hydrogen‑bond donors such as chiral phosphoric acids (BINOL‑derived) or thioureas activate electrophiles by binding to carbonyl oxygens or imine nitrogens, organising the transition state through a network of directional H‑bonds. These catalysts excel in Friedel‑Crafts-type reactions, transfer hydrogenations, and asymmetric Mannich reactions. The acidity of the phosphoric acid (pKₐ ≈ 3–4 in MeCN) is tuned by substituents on the 3,3′‑positions of the BINOL backbone, allowing fine control over reactivity and selectivity.

Dual catalysis combines two organocatalysts (or an organocatalyst with a metal catalyst) in a single pot. For example, a chiral phosphoric acid can activate an imine while a secondary amine forms an enamine from a carbonyl compound, enabling a highly enantioselective Mannich reaction. The orthogonal activation modes avoid catalyst interference and often permit lower catalyst loadings (0.5–5 mol %).

Organocatalysis has transformed the synthesis of complex natural products. The total synthesis of (−)-oseltamivir (Tamiflu) employs a proline‑catalysed asymmetric aldol reaction to set two contiguous stereocentres early in the sequence. The synthesis of (+)-allocyathin B₂ uses a chiral phosphoric acid to induce axial chirality in a biaryl axis via a Friedel‑Crafts cyclisation. These examples illustrate how organocatalytic steps can replace stoichiometric chiral auxiliaries, reducing step count and waste.

  • Assuming Norrish Type I and Type II pathways are mutually exclusive — both can operate simultaneously, and product ratios depend on conformation and substituents.
  • Treating all five‑membered heteroaromatics as equally reactive — pyrrole is far more electron‑rich than thiophene, altering regioselectivity and required reaction conditions.
  • Neglecting the need for a synthetic equivalent when a synthon is not directly purchasable — every disconnection must be paired with a realistic reagent.
  • Confusing atom economy with E‑factor — a reaction can have high atom economy yet a large E‑factor if solvents and work‑up materials dominate the mass balance.
  • Believing organocatalysts are universally compatible with all functional groups — strong acids, oxidants, or nucleophiles can deactivate the catalyst or lead to side reactions.
  • Overlooking the stereochemical consequence of iminium geometry — the E/Z ratio of the iminium intermediate directly influences the enantioselectivity of the subsequent nucleophilic attack.
  • Assuming green metrics are only relevant for industrial scale — exam questions increasingly ask for AE, E‑factor, or PMI calculations for laboratory‑scale routes.

These advanced topics deepen mechanistic insight and provide the quantitative tools needed for modern synthetic design and examination problem‑solving.

Radical Reaction Mechanisms: Initiation, Propagation, and Termination

Radical chain processes are governed by three distinct stages: initiation, propagation, and termination. Initiation creates the first radical species, typically by homolytic cleavage of a weak bond. Thermal decomposition of peroxides (e.g., benzoyl peroxide, Δ ≈ 130 °C) or azo compounds (AIBN, Δ ≈ 60 °C) yields two radicals per molecule. Photochemical initiation uses UV light to cleave halogen–halogen bonds (Cl₂ → 2 Cl·) or to excite carbonyl compounds to their n→π* state, which then undergo α‑cleavage (Norrish Type I). Redox initiation, common in atom‑transfer radical addition (ATRA), employs a transition‑metal complex (Cu(I)/Cu(II)) to generate a radical from an alkyl halide.

Propagation steps must be exergonic and fast enough to sustain a long chain. In the chlorination of methane, Cl· abstracts hydrogen (ΔH ≈ –1 kcal mol⁻¹) to give HCl and ·CH₃; the methyl radical then abstracts Cl from Cl₂ (ΔH ≈ –27 kcal mol⁻¹) regenerating Cl·. Chain length (average number of propagation cycles per initiation event) can exceed 10⁴ in efficient halogenations. Competing propagation pathways dictate regio‑ and stereoselectivity: in the addition of HBr to alkenes under peroxide conditions, the bromine radical adds to the less substituted carbon (anti‑Markovnikov) because the resulting carbon‑centered radical is more stabilized.

Termination removes radicals by combination (R· + R· → R–R) or disproportionation (R· + R'· → R–H + R'=). The relative rates of termination versus propagation control the molecular weight distribution in radical polymerizations. In controlled radical polymerizations (ATRP, RAFT), a reversible deactivation equilibrium suppresses termination, yielding narrow dispersity (Đ < 1.2). Side reactions such as β‑scission of alkoxy radicals (R–O· → carbonyl + alkyl·) or radical rearrangements (1,2‑shift) can divert the chain and must be considered when designing synthetic sequences.

Key synthetic applications include the Barton–McCombie deoxygenation (Bu₃SnH, AIBN) where a thiocarbonyl radical abstracts hydrogen to give a carbon radical that undergoes β‑scission, and the Hofmann–Löffler–Freytag reaction, where an N‑chloroamine generates a nitrogen‑centered radical that abstracts a δ‑hydrogen, leading to pyrrolidine formation after cyclization and reduction.

Carbene and Nitrene Chemistry: Generation, Insertion, and Stereochemical Control

Carbenes (neutral divalent carbon with six valence electrons) and nitrenes (nitrogen analogues) are powerful intermediates for C–H insertion, cyclopropanation, and aziridination. Singlet carbenes possess paired electrons in an sp² orbital and an empty p orbital, enabling concerted, stereospecific reactions. Triplet carbenes have two unpaired electrons and react stepwise via diradical pathways, often losing stereochemical fidelity. Generation methods dictate the spin state: thermolysis or photolysis of diazo compounds (e.g., diazomethane, ethyl diazoacetate) typically yields singlet carbenes, especially when a transition‑metal catalyst (Rh₂(OAc)₄, Cu(I) complexes) is present. The Simmons–Smith reagent (CH₂I₂/Zn(Cu)) delivers a carbenoid that behaves as a singlet methylene equivalent for cyclopropanation of alkenes with retention of alkene geometry.

Metal‑carbenoid C–H insertion proceeds through a concerted, asynchronous transition state where the C–H bond breaks as the new C–C bond forms. Rh(II) carboxylates favor insertion at the most electron‑rich, least hindered C–H bond (tertiary > secondary > primary) and exhibit high diastereoselectivity when a chiral ligand (e.g., Rh₂(S‑DOSP)₄) is used. Intramolecular insertion of α‑diazoesters into tethered C–H bonds constructs cyclic ethers and lactones with predictable ring sizes (5‑ and 6‑membered rings favored). Nitrenes are generated from azides (thermolysis, photolysis, or metal‑catalyzed decomposition of sulfonyl azides). Singlet nitrenes, accessed via Rh(II) or Cu(I) catalysis, undergo concerted C–H amination and aziridination with retention of configuration at the carbon center. Triplet nitrenes, formed by direct photolysis of aryl azides, abstract hydrogen atoms to give radical pairs that recombine, often leading to mixtures of regioisomers.

Stereochemical control in nitrene transfer is exemplified by the use of chiral dirhodium catalysts for enantioselective benzylic C–H amination (up to 98 % ee). In cyclopropanation, the geometry of the alkene is preserved when a singlet carbenoid adds (cis‑alkene → cis‑cyclopropane). However, with triplet carbenes or in the absence of a metal catalyst, loss of stereochemistry is observed due to the diradical intermediate's ability to rotate before ring closure.

Mechanistic Prediction
Predict the major product and its stereochemistry when (E)-4-phenyl-3-buten-2-yl diazoacetate is treated with Rh₂(S‑DOSP)₄ (1 mol %) in dichloromethane at 0 °C.
The substrate contains an α‑diazoester tethered to an (E)-alkene. Rh₂(S‑DOSP)₄ generates a singlet rhodium‑carbenoid that undergoes intramolecular C–H insertion preferentially at the allylic C–H bond α to the ester (a 5‑membered cyclic transition state). Because the carbenoid is singlet, the insertion is concerted and proceeds with retention of configuration at the reacting carbon. The (E)-alkene geometry is untouched. The product is a dihydrofuran‑2‑one (γ‑lactone) bearing a phenyl substituent at the 5‑position, formed as a single diastereomer with the newly created stereocenter defined by the chiral catalyst (typically >95 % ee). No cyclopropanation of the pendant alkene occurs because the 5‑exo‑trig C–H insertion is kinetically favored over 6‑endo‑trig cyclopropanation.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question presents a diazo compound and a chiral dirhodium catalyst, immediately identify the likely singlet carbenoid pathway. Check for competing 5‑exo‑trig versus 6‑endo‑trig cyclizations; the smaller ring usually wins. Remember that singlet carbenoids give stereospecific outcomes, while triplet pathways (direct photolysis, no metal) lead to loss of stereochemistry. Sketch the key transition state to verify ring size and stereochemical course before selecting an answer.

C–H Activation and Functionalization: Directing Groups and Catalytic Cycles

Transition‑metal‑catalyzed C–H activation converts inert C–H bonds directly into C–C or C–heteroatom bonds, bypassing pre‑functionalization. The reaction typically follows a catalytic cycle comprising (1) coordination of a directing group (DG) to the metal, (2) C–H cleavage via concerted metalation‑deprotonation (CMD), oxidative addition, or σ‑bond metathesis, (3) functionalization of the resulting organometallic intermediate (e.g., reductive elimination, transmetalation, or electrophilic trapping), and (4) catalyst regeneration. Common DGs include pyridines, amides, carboxylic acids, oxazolines, and nitriles; they bind through N or O atoms and position the metal ortho (or meta/para with tailored templates) to the target C–H bond.

Palladium(II/0) and Pd(II/IV) cycles dominate arylation and alkenylation. In the Fujiwara–Moritani reaction, a palladium(II) acetate catalyst activates an arene bearing a pyridine DG, undergoes CMD to give a palladacycle, then coordinates an alkene, inserts, and β‑hydride eliminates to afford the ortho‑alkenylated product. For alkylation, Pd(II/IV) pathways accessed by oxidants (PhI(OAc)₂, O₂) enable reductive elimination from a Pd(IV) center. Rhodium(III) catalysts (Cp*RhCl₂) operate via a Rh(III)/Rh(I) or Rh(III)/Rh(V) manifold, often employing carboxylate‑assisted CMD. Iridium(III) complexes with bidentate N,N‑ligands achieve meta‑selective C–H borylation using a nitrile‑based template that chelates the metal and directs it to the meta position through a seven‑membered cyclometalated intermediate.

Regioselectivity is governed by the DG's bite angle, the acidity of the targeted C–H bond, and steric hindrance. Electron‑rich arenes undergo faster CMD; electron‑deficient heterocycles may require more electrophilic metal centers (e.g., Pd(II) with Ag⁺ additives). Recent developments include transient directing groups (e.g., amino acids that form imines in situ) and ligand‑enabled remote functionalization (e.g., norbornene‑mediated ortho‑to‑para relay). Understanding the oxidation state changes and the nature of the C–H cleavage step is essential for predicting catalyst compatibility and for designing new transformations.

Kinetic Isotope Effects as Mechanistic Probes

Kinetic isotope effects (KIEs) quantify the change in reaction rate when an atom is replaced by its isotope, most commonly hydrogen by deuterium (k_H/k_D). A primary KIE (k_H/k_D > 2) indicates that the bond to the isotopically labeled atom is broken in the rate‑determining step (RDS). A secondary KIE (k_H/k_D ≈ 1.1–1.3) reflects changes in hybridization or hyperconjugation without bond cleavage. The magnitude of a primary KIE can distinguish between concerted and stepwise pathways: SN2 reactions typically show k_H/k_D ≈ 1.0–1.2 (no C–H bond breaking), whereas E2 eliminations exhibit k_H/k_D ≈ 3–7 because the β‑hydrogen is transferred in the RDS. In radical hydrogen‑atom abstractions, k_H/k_D values of 5–10 are common, and values >10 suggest quantum mechanical tunneling, especially at low temperature.

Experimental determination uses parallel reactions (separate runs with H and D substrates) or competitive reactions (single mixture, product ratio measured by NMR or GC‑MS). The competitive method eliminates systematic errors and yields the intrinsic KIE directly from the product isotope ratio. Intramolecular KIEs, where a single molecule contains both H and D at equivalent positions, provide the most rigorous data because they cancel out differences in concentration and catalyst loading.

Interpretation requires caution: a large KIE does not prove a specific mechanism; it only confirms that the labeled bond is cleaved in the RDS. For example, both SN1 ionization (C–X cleavage) and E1 elimination (C–H cleavage) can show modest KIEs if the RDS is carbocation formation. Combining KIE data with other probes (stereochemistry, radical clocks, Hammett ρ values) builds a coherent mechanistic picture. In enzyme catalysis, KIEs > 20 are taken as evidence for tunneling‑enhanced hydrogen transfer.

Flow Chemistry and Continuous Processing: Principles and Scale‑Up Strategies

Flow chemistry conducts reactions in a continuously pumped stream through a reactor (tube, chip, or packed column) rather than in a batch vessel. The high surface‑to‑volume ratio of microreactors (channel diameters 0.1–1 mm) enables rapid heat exchange (ΔT/Δt > 100 °C s⁻¹) and efficient mass transfer, allowing safe handling of highly exothermic or hazardous transformations (e.g., diazomethane generation, nitrations, organolithium chemistry). Residence time (τ = V_reactor / Q_flow) replaces reaction time as the key kinetic parameter; precise control of τ yields narrow product distributions and suppresses over‑reaction.

Telescoping multiple steps without intermediate isolation is a major advantage: the effluent of a first reactor can be mixed with a second reagent stream and passed directly into a downstream reactor. Inline analytical tools (FT‑IR, UV‑Vis, NMR, mass spectrometry) provide real‑time conversion data, enabling feedback control of flow rates or temperature. Scale‑up is achieved by numbering‑up (parallelizing identical reactor channels) rather than increasing vessel volume, preserving the favorable transport phenomena. Process mass intensity (PMI) and E‑factor often improve because solvent volumes are reduced and work‑up steps are minimized.

Typical applications include the continuous synthesis of active pharmaceutical ingredients (APIs) such as ibuprofen (three‑step flow sequence, overall yield > 80 %), the generation and immediate consumption of unstable intermediates (e.g., benzyne, nitrenes), and photochemical reactions where uniform irradiation of a thin film overcomes the penetration depth limitation of batch reactors. Integration with downstream purification (continuous extraction, crystallization, or chromatography) creates fully automated end‑to‑end manufacturing lines.

  • Assuming all radical reactions proceed via a single chain length — chain length varies widely with initiator concentration, temperature, and solvent polarity.
  • Treating singlet and triplet carbenes as interchangeable — their reactivity and stereochemical outcomes differ fundamentally.
  • Believing any directing group works for every metal — DG‑metal compatibility (e.g., pyridine for Pd, amide for Rh) is crucial.
  • Confusing primary and secondary KIEs — only primary KIEs directly report on bond cleavage in the RDS.
  • Thinking flow scale‑up simply means larger tubing — numbering‑up preserves microreactor advantages; larger diameters degrade heat/mass transfer.
  • Overlooking the role of reversible deactivation in controlled radical polymerizations — without it, termination dominates and dispersity broadens.
  • Neglecting that nitrene spin state depends on generation method — metal‑catalyzed decomposition favors singlet nitrenes, direct photolysis often gives triplets.

These advanced topics provide the mechanistic depth and practical insight required for high‑level problem solving in modern organic chemistry examinations.

Metal‑Catalyzed Cross‑Coupling Mechanisms: Oxidative Addition, Transmetalation, and Reductive Elimination

Modern cross‑coupling reactions rely on a catalytic cycle that can be dissected into three fundamental elementary steps. Oxidative addition inserts a low‑valent metal centre (typically Pd⁰ or Ni⁰) into a carbon–halogen or carbon–pseudohalogen bond, generating a metal‑aryl (or vinyl, alkyl) intermediate in a higher oxidation state. The rate of this step is governed by the electronic nature of the aryl halide (electron‑deficient aryl bromides and iodides add fastest), the steric bulk of the ligand sphere, and the metal’s intrinsic redox potential; for example, Pd(PPh₃)₄ adds to iodobenzene at 25 °C within minutes, whereas the same catalyst requires 80 °C for chlorobenzene unless a strongly electron‑donating, bulky phosphine such as Xantphos or a N‑heterocyclic carbene is employed. Transmetalation transfers the organic fragment from a main‑group organometallic reagent (boronate in Suzuki–Miyaura, stannane in Stille, zinc reagent in Negishi, or copper‑acetylide in Sonogashira) to the metal centre. This step often proceeds via a four‑centered transition state in which a base (e.g., Na₂CO₃, K₃PO₄) activates the boronate to a more nucleophilic boronate‑ate complex; the choice of base and solvent (aqueous/ethanol mixtures for Suzuki, anhydrous THF for Negishi) critically influences the rate and suppresses protodeboronation. Reductive elimination from the diorganometal intermediate forms the new C–C (or C–N, C–O) bond and regenerates the low‑valent catalyst. The barrier for reductive elimination drops dramatically when the two carbon ligands are cis‑oriented and when the metal bears electron‑rich, sterically demanding ligands that favour a three‑coordinate transition state; this principle underlies the success of Buchwald‑Hartwig aminations with bulky biaryl phosphines. Nickel catalysis follows an analogous cycle but often accesses Niᴵ/Niᴵᴵᴵ manifolds, enabling coupling of unactivated alkyl electrophiles that undergo rapid β‑hydride elimination on palladium. Key exceptions include the “oxidative addition‑reductive elimination” pathway for aryl chlorides with Ni⁰/NHC complexes, and the “radical‑relay” mechanism observed in photoredox‑enabled cross‑couplings where a single‑electron transfer generates an aryl radical that adds to Ni⁰. Understanding the interplay of ligand electronics, halide leaving‑group ability, and the oxidation state manifold allows the chemist to predict whether a given substrate pair will couple efficiently or require a tailored catalyst system.

Photoredox Catalysis: Single‑Electron Transfer Pathways and Synthetic Applications

Photoredox catalysis exploits visible‑light absorption by transition‑metal complexes (most commonly Ir(ppy)₃, Ru(bpy)₃²⁺, and organic dyes such as 4CzIPN) to generate long‑lived excited states that act as potent single‑electron oxidants or reductants. Upon irradiation, the metal‑to‑ligand charge‑transfer (MLCT) state of Ir(ppy)₃ has an excited‑state reduction potential of approximately –1.73 V vs SCE (reductive quenching) and an oxidation potential of +0.77 V (oxidative quenching), enabling it to engage a broad spectrum of organic substrates. In a reductive quenching cycle, a sacrificial electron donor (e.g., triethylamine, Hantzsch ester) reduces the excited catalyst to its Irᴵᴵ form, which then transfers an electron to an electron‑deficient acceptor such as an aryl halide, generating a radical anion that fragments to give an aryl radical. Conversely, oxidative quenching uses an electron‑rich substrate (e.g., an enolate, a tertiary amine) to oxidize the excited catalyst to Irᴵⱽ, which can then oxidize a second substrate or undergo energy transfer. The choice between oxidative and reductive quenching is dictated by the relative redox potentials of the substrates and the catalyst’s excited‑state potentials; a substrate with an oxidation potential lower than +0.77 V will be oxidatively quenched, while one with a reduction potential more positive than –1.73 V will be reductively quenched. Representative transformations include the decarboxylative alkylation of redox‑active esters (N‑hydroxyphthalimide esters) where the Irᴵᴵ catalyst reduces the ester to a carboxyl‑radical anion that loses CO₂, the resulting alkyl radical adding to an alkene or being trapped by a nickel catalyst for cross‑coupling. Another hallmark is the α‑functionalization of carbonyl compounds via hydrogen‑atom transfer (HAT) from a thiol co‑catalyst, where the excited Irᴵⱽ oxidizes the thiol to a thiyl radical that abstracts an α‑hydrogen. Quantum yields for these processes are often modest (Φ ≈ 0.1–0.5) because of competing back‑electron transfer, but the use of continuous‑flow photoreactors with high photon flux can raise effective turnover numbers. Recent advances involve dual catalysis, merging photoredox with nickel, copper, or organocatalysis to achieve enantioselective C–C bond formation, and the development of organic photocatalysts that avoid precious metals while retaining strong reducing power (e.g., phenothiazine derivatives with E₁/₂* ≈ –2.1 V). Mastery of the redox ladder, excited‑state lifetimes, and the interplay of quenching pathways is essential for designing efficient photoredox sequences.

Mechanistic Analysis
A reaction mixture contains Ir(ppy)₃ (1 mol %), an aryl bromide (Ar–Br), a tertiary amine (DIPEA), and an alkene. Under blue LED irradiation, the major product is the alkyl‑aryl coupled product Ar–CH₂–CH₂–R. Propose the dominant quenching pathway, the key radical intermediate, and the role of the amine.
The excited Ir(ppy)₃* (E₁/₂* ≈ –1.73 V) is reductively quenched by DIPEA (oxidation potential ≈ +0.78 V), generating Irᴵᴵ and the DIPEA radical cation. Irᴵᴵ reduces the aryl bromide (E₁/₂ ≈ –1.5 V) to an aryl radical anion, which fragments to give the aryl radical Ar·. This radical adds to the alkene to form a new carbon‑centered radical, which is then reduced by a second equivalent of Irᴵᴵ (or by a Hantzsch ester if present) to an anion that is protonated, delivering the coupled product. The amine thus serves as a sacrificial electron donor to close the catalytic cycle.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a photoredox question appears, first list the excited‑state redox potentials of the catalyst given in the problem. Compare each substrate’s ground‑state oxidation or reduction potential to those values; the pathway with the most favorable thermodynamic driving force is the one the examiner expects. Sketch the catalytic cycle with oxidation states and electron flow — this visual check often reveals missing steps such as a second reduction or a proton transfer that earns full marks.

Dynamic Kinetic Resolution and Deracemization Strategies

Dynamic kinetic resolution (DKR) converts a racemic mixture into a single enantiomerically enriched product by coupling a rapid, reversible racemization of the starting material with an irreversible, enantioselective transformation of one enantiomer. The classic implementation pairs an enzyme (often a lipase or an acyltransferase) that acylates only the (R)‑alcohol with a metal‑catalyzed racemization of the (S)‑alcohol via a transient ketone intermediate. Ruthenium‑Shvo’s catalyst or a palladium‑diamine complex can effect the dehydrogenation/hydrogenation sequence that interconverts the enantiomers on a timescale faster than the enzymatic acylation, ensuring that the (S)‑enantiomer is continuously funneled into the reactive (R)‑pool. The overall enantiomeric excess (ee) of the product is dictated by the relative rates: k_rac ≫ k_cat · [enzyme] for high ee. A practical example is the DKR of 1‑phenylethanol using Candida antarctica lipase B (CALB) and a Ru‑Shvo catalyst in toluene at 60 °C, delivering the (R)‑acetate in >99 % ee and >95 % yield. Deracemization extends this concept to solid‑state processes: a racemic conglomerate is subjected to a chiral additive that selectively inhibits the growth of one crystal enantiomorph while a racemization catalyst (often a base or a metal complex) equilibrates the solution phase. The “crystallization‑induced asymmetric transformation” (CIAT) of α‑amino acids using a chiral auxiliary and a palladium‑catalyzed racemization exemplifies this, delivering enantiopure product after a single crystallization. Key parameters include the solubility ratio of the enantiomers, the rate of racemization in the mother liquor, and the chiral additive’s binding constant. Failure modes arise when racemization is slower than crystallization (leading to incomplete conversion) or when the enzyme loses activity in the presence of the metal catalyst (necessitating biphasic or immobilized systems). Recent advances employ photochemical racemization (e.g., visible‑light‑mediated hydrogen‑atom transfer) to avoid metal contamination, and engineered enzymes with broadened substrate scope for industrial DKR of β‑substituted alcohols.

Organic Electrochemistry: Redox Mechanisms, Electrode Materials, and Scale‑Up Considerations

Electrochemical synthesis drives organic transformations by passing current through an electrolyte, effecting oxidation at the anode and reduction at the cathode without stoichiometric chemical oxidants or reductants. The fundamental mechanistic step is electron transfer between the electrode surface and a substrate, generating a radical cation (anodic) or radical anion (cathodic) that undergoes subsequent bond formation or cleavage. Anodic oxidation of carboxylates (Kolbe electrolysis) yields alkyl radicals that dimerize; the reaction rate depends on the electrode potential relative to the substrate’s oxidation potential and on the electrode material’s overpotential for oxygen evolution. Glassy carbon, boron‑doped diamond (BDD), and platinum are common anodes; BDD offers a wide potential window (>3 V) and low adsorption, making it ideal for oxidizing electron‑rich arenes to radical cations that undergo C–C coupling. Cathodic reductions include the Birch‑type reduction of aromatic rings in liquid ammonia, the reductive cleavage of aryl halides to aryl anions (which can be trapped with electrophiles), and the electrochemical generation of organometallic reagents (e.g., in situ formation of Grignard‑type species from alkyl halides at a magnesium anode). Paired electrolysis exploits both half‑reactions simultaneously: the anodic oxidation of a thiol to a disulfide can be paired with the cathodic reduction of an alkyl halide to a radical that adds to an alkene, delivering two valuable products per electron passed. Mediated electrochemistry introduces a redox‑active catalyst (e.g., TEMPO, quinuclidine, or a nickel complex) that shuttles electrons between the electrode and the substrate, lowering the required cell voltage and improving selectivity. Scale‑up moves from batch “beaker” cells to flow electrolyzers with narrow inter‑electrode gaps (0.1–0.5 mm) and high surface‑area electrodes (porous carbon felts, mesh). The key engineering parameters are current density (typically 10–100 mA cm⁻²), residence time (τ = V/Q), and electrolyte conductivity (supported by tetraalkylammonium salts). Heat removal is efficient due to the high surface‑to‑volume ratio, allowing exothermic oxidations such as the Shono oxidation of N‑protected amines to be run safely at multi‑gram scale. Product isolation often benefits from the absence of metal salts; simple aqueous work‑up or continuous extraction suffices. Understanding the interplay of electrode kinetics (Butler‑Volmer behavior), mass transport (diffusion layer thickness), and the thermodynamic redox potentials of all species enables the design of electrochemical routes that are both atom‑economical and scalable.

  • Assuming a single catalytic cycle applies to all cross‑coupling partners — oxidative addition rates differ dramatically between aryl iodides, bromides, and chlorides, and nickel vs palladium manifolds.
  • Neglecting the distinction between oxidative and reductive quenching in photoredox cycles — the excited‑state redox potentials dictate which substrate donates or accepts the electron.
  • Treating DKR as a simple kinetic resolution — without a racemization rate faster than the enantioselective step, the maximum yield is 50 %.
  • Overlooking electrode overpotentials — a substrate may be thermodynamically oxidizable but kinetically hindered on a given electrode material.
  • Confusing mediated electrochemistry with direct electrolysis — mediators change the effective potential and can introduce side reactions if not matched to substrate potentials.
  • Believing that flow electrolysis automatically improves selectivity — residence‑time distribution and mass‑transport limitations must still be optimized.
  • Ignoring the effect of ligand sterics on reductive elimination — bulky biaryl phosphines accelerate C–C bond formation but can impede oxidative addition for hindered substrates.

These advanced mechanistic insights and practical considerations form the core of modern synthetic problem solving in organic chemistry examinations.

Supramolecular Chemistry and Host‑Guest Interactions

Fundamental Principles

Supramolecular chemistry studies non‑covalent assemblies held together by hydrogen bonds, π‑π stacking, electrostatic forces, van der Waals contacts, and hydrophobic effects. The central design criteria are complementarity (geometric and electronic match between host and guest), preorganization (host adopts a binding‑competent conformation without large reorganization energy), and cooperativity (binding of one guest influences subsequent binding events). Thermodynamic signatures are obtained from isothermal titration calorimetry (ITC), giving ΔH, ΔS, and the binding constant Ka. Typical Ka values range from 102 M−1 for weak inclusion complexes to >107 M−1 for high‑affinity cucurbituril–guest pairs.

Classic Host Families

Crown ethers (e.g., 18‑crown‑6) selectively bind alkali metal cations; the cavity size matches K⁺ (1.38 Å) giving Ka ≈ 106 M−1 in methanol. Cyclodextrins (α, β, γ) are cyclic oligosaccharides with a hydrophobic interior; β‑cyclodextrin forms inclusion complexes with aromatic guests (Ka 103–104 M−1) and is widely used for drug solubilisation. Calixarenes are phenolic macrocycles that can be functionalised at the upper and lower rims, enabling ion‑pair recognition and chiral discrimination. Cucurbit[n]urils (n = 5–8) possess a rigid, barrel‑shaped cavity with carbonyl portals that bind cationic guests via ion‑dipole interactions; cucurbit[7]uril binds ferrocene derivatives with Ka > 1012 M−1 in water.

Applications in Sensing, Separation, and Catalysis

Host‑guest chemistry underpins fluorescent sensors (e.g., displacement assays where a fluorescent dye is ejected from a cyclodextrin by the analyte), enantiomeric separation on chiral stationary phases derived from cyclodextrins, and supramolecular catalysis where a catalyst is encapsulated in a cage to alter selectivity. Metal‑organic cages built from Pd(II) or Fe(II) nodes and ditopic ligands create confined reaction vessels that accelerate Diels–Alder reactions by enforcing a near‑attack conformation. In all cases, the binding constant, exchange kinetics, and solvent competition dictate practical performance.

Experimental Determination of Binding Parameters

NMR titration (chemical‑shift changes of host or guest protons) yields Ka via non‑linear fitting. UV‑Vis or fluorescence titration provides an alternative when chromophores are present. ITC directly measures heat of binding, delivering ΔH and Ka in a single experiment. Job plots (continuous variation) confirm stoichiometry. For very tight binders (Ka > 108 M−1), competitive displacement with a known weaker guest is employed.

Design Strategies for Enhanced Selectivity

Introducing secondary coordination sites (e.g., urea groups on calixarene upper rim) creates multi‑point recognition. Allosteric regulation is mimicked by linking two binding sites through a flexible spacer; guest binding at one site induces a conformational change that modulates affinity at the second site. Computational docking (MM‑GBSA, DFT‑optimised host‑guest complexes) guides synthesis by predicting binding modes and estimating ΔGbind before laboratory work.

Biocatalysis and Enzyme Engineering in Organic Synthesis

Enzyme Classes Relevant to Synthesis

The six IUBMB classes provide a framework: oxidoreductases (alcohol dehydrogenases, ketoreductases, transaminases, cytochrome P450 monooxygenases), hydrolases (lipases, esterases, epoxide hydrolases), transferases (glycosyltransferases, methyltransferases), lyases (aldolases, decarboxylases), isomerases (epimerases, racemases), and ligases (ATP‑dependent carboxylases). Oxidoreductases and hydrolases dominate industrial biocatalysis because they operate under mild aqueous conditions and deliver high enantioselectivity.

Directed Evolution and Rational Design

Directed evolution mimics natural selection: a gene library (error‑prone PCR, DNA shuffling) is expressed, and variants are screened for improved activity, stability, or selectivity. The 2018 Nobel‑winning work on cytochrome P450 BM3 variants illustrates >100‑fold rate enhancements for non‑native substrates. Rational design uses crystal structures or homology models to target residues in the active site, cofactor binding pocket, or surface loops. Computational tools (Rosetta, FoldX) predict ΔΔG of mutation, guiding library size reduction.

Cofactor Regeneration Strategies

Many oxidoreductases require NAD(P)H or NAD(P)⁺. Stoichiometric cofactor use is uneconomical; therefore, in‑situ regeneration is essential. Common systems: (i) glucose dehydrogenase (GDH) with glucose → gluconolactone, regenerating NADPH; (ii) formate dehydrogenase (FDH) with sodium formate → CO₂, regenerating NADH; (iii) phosphite dehydrogenase (PTDH) with phosphite → phosphate. Enzyme‑coupled regeneration achieves >99 % cofactor turnover at <0.5 mol % cofactor loading.

Immobilisation and Process Integration

Immobilisation on epoxy‑activated resins, silica, or magnetic nanoparticles improves enzyme reusability, enables continuous‑flow operation, and simplifies product isolation. Cross‑linked enzyme aggregates (CLEAs) combine purification and immobilisation in one step. In flow reactors, residence time distribution is narrow, allowing precise control of conversion and minimising product inhibition. Integrated cascades (e.g., transaminase → ketoreductase → alcohol dehydrogenase) achieve multi‑step synthesis without intermediate isolation.

Industrial Case Studies

Codexis engineered a transaminase for sitagliptin manufacture, replacing a high‑pressure rhodium‑catalysed asymmetric hydrogenation; the biocatalytic route delivers >99.9 % ee, 56 % overall yield, and eliminates metal waste. Merck’s ketoreductase process for the HIV integrase inhibitor MK‑8591 uses a cofactor‑regeneration loop with GDH, achieving 200 g L−1 product titer. These examples illustrate how enzyme engineering translates to greener, cheaper manufacturing.

Technique: Designing a Cofactor Regeneration System
A process chemist must choose a regeneration method for NADPH in a ketoreductase‑catalysed asymmetric reduction of a prochiral ketone to a chiral alcohol at 100 g L−1 substrate loading. Compare glucose dehydrogenase (GDH) and phosphite dehydrogenase (PTDH) in terms of atom economy, by‑product handling, and enzyme stability.
Solution. GDH uses glucose (C₆H₁₂O₆) → gluconolactone (C₆H₁₀O₆) + 2 H⁺ + 2 e⁻, consuming one glucose per NADPH regenerated. Atom economy = (MW of NADPH regenerated) / (MW glucose + MW NADPH) ≈ 744 / (180 + 744) ≈ 80 %. The by‑product gluconolactone hydrolyses to gluconic acid, which is water‑soluble and can be removed by ion‑exchange. GDH is robust (Topt 30–40 °C, pH 7–8) and commercially available. PTDH uses phosphite (HPO₃²⁻) → phosphate (HPO₄²⁻), atom economy ≈ 744 / (80 + 744) ≈ 90 %. Phosphate is benign but accumulates; high ionic strength may affect downstream purification. PTDH often shows lower thermal stability (Topt 25–35 °C) and higher cost. For large‑scale aqueous processes, GDH/glucose is preferred due to cheaper substrate, easier waste handling, and proven industrial track record.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks to compare biocatalytic vs. chemical routes, structure the answer around three pillars: (1) selectivity (enantio‑, regio‑, chemoselectivity), (2) cofactor/metal cost and recycling, (3) process metrics (E‑factor, PMI, space‑time yield). Cite a concrete industrial example (sitagliptin, MK‑8591) to earn full marks.

Computational Mechanistic Studies: DFT and Kinetic Modeling

Choice of Functional and Basis Set

Modern mechanistic work relies on hybrid functionals with dispersion corrections. M06‑2X and ωB97X‑D are widely benchmarked for main‑group thermochemistry and barrier heights; B3LYP‑D3BJ remains popular for organometallic systems when combined with a triple‑ζ basis (def2‑TZVP) and an effective core potential for transition metals. Solvation is modelled implicitly (SMD, PCM) with the experimental solvent (e.g., THF, MeCN). Explicit solvent molecules are added for hydrogen‑bonding networks or ion‑pairing.

Locating Transition States and Verifying Pathways

A transition state (TS) is a first‑order saddle point on the potential energy surface. Geometry optimisation uses the Berny algorithm with a TS guess from a relaxed scan or the QST2/QST3 methods. Frequency analysis confirms a single imaginary frequency corresponding to the reaction coordinate. Intrinsic reaction coordinate (IRC) calculations trace the minimum‑energy path from TS to reactants and products, ensuring the correct connectivity.

Free‑Energy Profiles and Rate Constants

Standard state corrections (1 M, 298 K) convert electronic energies to Gibbs free energies (ΔG‡). Eyring equation k = (kBT/h) exp(−ΔG‡/RT) yields rate constants. For multi‑step sequences, microkinetic modelling (solving coupled ODEs) identifies the rate‑determining step (RDS) and predicts concentration‑time profiles. Sensitivity analysis reveals which barriers most affect overall turnover frequency (TOF).

Predicting Kinetic Isotope Effects (KIEs)

Primary KIEs arise from zero‑point energy differences in bonds broken/formed at the TS. Computed vibrational frequencies for isotopologues (e.g., H vs D) give the KIE via the Bigeleisen‑Mayer equation. Agreement between calculated and experimental KIEs (typically 2–7 for C–H cleavage) validates the proposed TS geometry. Secondary KIEs (≈1.1–1.3) probe hybridisation changes.

Limitations and Best Practices

Functional dependence can shift barriers by 2–4 kcal mol−1; therefore, single‑point energy refinement with a higher‑level method (DLPNO‑CCSD(T), double‑hybrid functional) on the DFT geometry is recommended. Entropic contributions for low‑frequency modes (<100 cm−1) are often overestimated; quasi‑harmonic or Grimme’s free‑rotor corrections improve reliability. Always report the functional, basis set, solvation model, and thermal corrections to enable reproducibility.

Late‑Stage Functionalization Strategies in Complex Molecule Synthesis

Strategic Value of Late‑Stage Diversification

Late‑stage functionalization (LSF) modifies a complex, often advanced intermediate without de‑novo synthesis. It accelerates analogue generation for structure‑activity‑relationship (SAR) studies, reduces step count, and preserves stereochemical integrity. The key challenge is achieving chemoselectivity amidst dense functionality (heterocycles, amides, sulfonamides, halides).

Directed C–H Activation with Transient Directing Groups

Transient directing groups (TDGs) such as 2‑pyridyl‑silyl ethers or amino‑acid‑derived auxiliaries coordinate to Pd(II), Ir(III), or Rh(III) catalysts, enabling ortho‑C–H arylation/alkylation of arenes and heteroarenes. After functionalization, the TDG is removed under mild conditions (fluoride for silyl, hydrolysis for amides). This approach avoids permanent directing‑group installation and removal steps, improving step economy.

Photoredox‑Mediated Radical Functionalization

Visible‑light photoredox catalysts (Ir(ppy)₃, 4CzIPN) generate radicals from alkyl halides, redox‑active esters (NHPI esters), or Katritzky salts. Dual catalysis with Ni(0)/Ni(II) enables cross‑electrophile coupling of two radical precursors at late stage. The mild conditions tolerate acid‑labile protecting groups and enable C(sp³)–C(sp²) bond formation on densely functionalised scaffolds (e.g., Merck’s fluorination of a kinase inhibitor core).

Electrochemical Oxidation for Heteroaryl Coupling

Anodic oxidation of electron‑rich heteroarenes (indoles, pyrroles) generates radical cations that couple with nucleophiles (enamides, silyl enol ethers) without metal catalysts. Paired electrolysis can simultaneously reduce an aryl halide at the cathode, delivering two C–C bonds per electron pair. Flow electrolyzers with boron‑doped diamond anodes provide scalable, safe operation.

Case Study: Rapid Analogue Generation for a Clinical Candidate

A pharmaceutical program employed a Ni/photoredox dual catalytic system to install diverse alkyl groups at a C‑7 position of a tricyclic core in a single step from a common bromide precursor. Over 30 analogues were prepared in 48 h, enabling identification of a candidate with improved pharmacokinetic properties. The LSF step proceeded in 65 % isolated yield, with no protecting‑group manipulation, illustrating the power of modern radical cross‑coupling.

Metal‑Free Catalysis: Frustrated Lewis Pairs and Main‑Group Activation

Concept of Frustrated Lewis Pairs (FLPs)

A frustrated Lewis pair consists of a sterically hindered Lewis acid (LA) and a Lewis base (LB) that cannot form a classical adduct due to steric clash. The archetypal FLP is B(C₆F₅)₃ (LA) combined with tBu₃P (LB). The unquenched acidity/basicity enables cooperative activation of small molecules: H₂, CO₂, N₂O, alkenes, and alkynes. The heterolytic cleavage of H₂ yields a phosphonium hydride (tBu₃PH⁺) and a borohydride (HB(C₆F₅)₃⁻), which can hydrogenate imines, enamines, and activated alkenes under 1–10 bar H₂ at 25–80 °C.

Catalytic Cycles and Turnover Frequencies

In FLP‑catalysed hydrogenation, the LA/LB pair first splits H₂; the resulting ion pair transfers hydride to the substrate’s electrophilic centre (e.g., iminium carbon) while the phosphonium protonates the nitrogen. Regeneration of the free FLP occurs by deprotonation of the phosphonium by the borohydride. Reported turnover frequencies (TOFs) for imine hydrogenation reach 10⁴ h⁻¹ with 0.5 mol % FLP loading. Catalyst deactivation pathways include irreversible adduct formation with strong bases or oxidation of the phosphine.

Main‑Group Catalysts Beyond FLPs

Low‑valent main‑group compounds (e.g., Al(I) β‑diketiminate complexes, Ga(I) N‑heterocyclic carbene adducts, silylenes) undergo oxidative addition into E–H (E = B, Si, Ge) bonds, mimicking transition‑metal reactivity.
