Explain the mechanistic origin of this change, focusing on the role of the carbonate counterion and solvent polarity.
In the biphasic toluene/water system, Na₂CO₃ is partially soluble in the aqueous phase, generating a high concentration of free carbonate anion that can coordinate to the Pd(II)‑aryl intermediate as a weakly bound ligand, facilitating transmetalation by delivering the boronate to the metal center. The aqueous phase also promotes hydrolysis of the boronic acid to the more nucleophilic boronate. In pure toluene with Cs₂CO₃, the carbonate is poorly solvated and exists as tight ion pairs with Cs⁺; the resulting Cs₂CO₃ is essentially insoluble, so the concentration of free carbonate is negligible. Without a coordinating anion, the Pd(II)‑aryl‑Br complex remains coordinatively saturated, slowing transmetalation. The low polarity of toluene further disfavors ionization of the Pd–Br bond, leading to accumulation of the Pd(II)‑aryl‑Br species that undergoes reductive elimination to give the homocoupled biaryl. Thus, the counterion’s ability to act as a transient ligand and the solvent’s dielectric constant together control the transmetalation rate and the competition between cross‑coupling and homocoupling pathways.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks you to rationalize a change in yield or selectivity upon swapping a base or solvent, first identify the counterion’s coordinating ability and the solvent’s polarity. Sketch the catalytic cycle, mark where the anion can bind, and predict whether the step is accelerated (labile anion, polar solvent) or retarded (tight ion pair, non‑polar solvent). This systematic approach turns a qualitative observation into a mechanistic argument that examiners reward.

Organic Electrochemical Reductive Couplings: Paired Electrolysis and Redox‑Neutral Synthesis

Paired electrolysis exploits simultaneous cathodic reduction and anodic oxidation in a single electrochemical cell, achieving overall redox‑neutral transformations without stoichiometric chemical oxidants or reductants. In a typical reductive coupling, an aryl halide (Ar–X) is reduced at the cathode to an aryl radical anion that fragments to an aryl radical and X⁻; the radical adds to an alkene or undergoes radical–radical coupling with a second electrogenerated radical. Concurrently, a sacrificial anodic reaction (e.g., oxidation of a benzylic ether to a carbonyl, or oxidation of a carboxylate to CO₂ and a radical) balances charge. The choice of electrode material (graphite, Pt, Ni, reticulated vitreous carbon) influences overpotential and selectivity: graphite favors organic radical generation, while Pt can catalyze hydrogen evolution, lowering Faradaic efficiency. Mediated electrolysis uses a redox‑active mediator (e.g., anthraquinone, TEMPO, or a cobalt‑salen complex) that shuttles electrons between the electrode and the substrate, enabling reactions at lower applied potentials and improving functional‑group tolerance. In a landmark example, a nickel‑catalyzed cross‑electrophile coupling of aryl bromides with alkyl iodides was performed under paired electrolysis: the cathode reduces Ni(II) to Ni(0) and generates alkyl radicals from the alkyl iodide; the anode oxidizes a sacrificial amine to an iminium ion that is trapped by a nucleophile, delivering a value‑added by‑product. Key mechanistic parameters include the cathodic peak potential of the electrophile (measured by cyclic voltammetry), the rate of radical capture by the metal catalyst versus dimerization, and the anodic stability of the mediator. Mass‑transport limitations become critical at current densities >10 mA cm⁻²; flow cells with narrow inter‑electrode gaps (≤0.5 mm) and high flow rates (≥5 mL min⁻¹) mitigate concentration polarization. Recent advances employ divided cells with anion‑exchange membranes to prevent crossover of reactive intermediates, enabling truly selective paired processes such as the simultaneous synthesis of a chiral amine (cathodic reductive amination) and a carboxylic acid (anodic oxidation of a primary alcohol).

Catalytic Asymmetric C–H Functionalization with Chiral Directing Groups

Directing‑group‑assisted C–H activation has been rendered enantioselective by attaching a chiral, removable auxiliary to the substrate. The most widely used chiral directing groups (CDGs) are derived from oxazolines, 8‑aminoquinoline amides bearing a chiral substituent at the 2‑position, and N‑acetyl‑amino‑indane scaffolds. In a typical palladium‑catalyzed β‑C–H arylation of a chiral amide, the CDG chelates Pd(II) in a bidentate fashion, forming a five‑membered palladacycle that positions the target C–H bond for concerted metalation‑deprotonation (CMD). The chiral environment of the CDG biases the conformation of the palladacycle, so that the subsequent reductive elimination with an aryl‑iodide occurs from a single diastereomeric transition state, delivering the product with high enantiomeric excess (often >95 % ee). After functionalization, the CDG is cleaved under mild conditions (e.g., LiAlH₄ reduction of an oxazoline to the corresponding amino alcohol, or hydrolytic removal of an 8‑aminoquinoline amide). Iridium‑catalyzed asymmetric borylation of arenes employs a chiral N‑heterocyclic carbene (NHC) ligand on Ir together with a transient chiral directing group (a chiral phosphoric acid that hydrogen‑bonds to the substrate), achieving enantioselective C–H borylation without a covalently attached auxiliary. Rhodium‑catalyzed asymmetric C–H amination uses a chiral Cp*Rh(III) complex and a chiral sulfonyl azide as the nitrene source; the chiral ligand controls the facial approach of the nitrene to the rhodium‑bound arene. Key challenges include the need for stoichiometric amounts of the CDG (unless a catalytic, transient directing group is used), the potential for epimerization at the stereocenter of the CDG under the reaction conditions, and the limited substrate scope imposed by the requirement for a coordinating heteroatom. Recent developments address these issues by designing traceless directing groups that undergo in‑situ fragmentation after C–H functionalization, and by employing chiral counterions (e.g., BINOL‑derived phosphates) that associate with the cationic metal center to induce asymmetry without covalent modification of the substrate.

Mechanistic Studies of Radical Cascade Reactions in Complex Molecule Synthesis

Radical cascade (or domino) reactions construct multiple bonds and stereocenters in a single operation by sequential radical additions, cyclizations, and fragmentations. A classic example is the tin‑mediated cascade of a 1,6‑enyne bearing a pendant alkyl halide: Bu₃SnH initiates a radical at the halide, which adds to the alkyne to give a vinyl radical; 5‑exo‑trig cyclization onto the alkene forms a new C–C bond and a secondary carbon radical that undergoes β‑scission of a neighboring C–O bond, releasing a carbonyl fragment and generating a stabilized radical that is finally quenched by hydrogen atom transfer from Bu₃SnH. The stereochemical outcome is governed by the conformational preferences of the cyclic transition states (chair‑like for 6‑endo‑trig, boat‑like for 5‑exo‑trig) and by the relative rates of competing cyclization modes (Baldwin’s rules). Modern tin‑free cascades employ photoredox catalysis (Ir(ppy)₃, blue LEDs) to generate the initial radical from a redox‑active ester (e.g., N‑hydroxyphthalimide ester) under mild conditions; the subsequent radical cascade proceeds without stoichiometric tin, improving functional‑group tolerance. Mechanistic elucidation relies on a combination of techniques: (i) radical clocks (cyclopropyl‑carbinyl radicals) to measure the lifetime of intermediate radicals; (ii) EPR spectroscopy with spin traps (DMPO, PBN) to detect transient radicals; (iii) kinetic isotope effects (KIEs) measured by comparing rates of H‑ vs D‑atom transfer steps; (iv) DFT calculations of the free‑energy profiles for each cyclization pathway, including solvent effects (SMD model). A common pitfall is assuming that all steps are irreversible; in many cascades, the first cyclization is reversible, and the product distribution is under Curtin–Hammett control. Another subtlety is the role of hydrogen‑atom donors: thiols (PhSH) give faster HAT than silanes (PhSiH₃) but can also add to alkenes, diverting the cascade. Designing a successful cascade therefore requires balancing the rates of radical generation, cyclization, fragmentation, and termination, often guided by computational prediction of the highest‑energy transition state.

  • Assuming that a chiral directing group always transfers its stereochemistry faithfully — epimerization at the auxiliary’s stereocenter under the reaction conditions can erode enantioselectivity.
  • Treating paired electrolysis as a simple sum of two half‑reactions — crossover of reactive intermediates across the membrane can lead to undesired side reactions unless a divided cell is used.
  • Overlooking the reversibility of the first cyclization in a radical cascade — product ratios may reflect Curtin–Hammett kinetics rather than the relative rates of forward steps.
  • Neglecting the coordinating ability of the counterion in cationic organometallic catalysis — a weakly coordinating anion can accelerate transmetalation, while a strongly coordinating one can inhibit it.
  • Assuming that all asymmetric hydrofunctionalizations proceed via a single metal‑hydride insertion pathway — electron‑deficient alkenes can engage radical pathways that scramble stereochemistry.
  • Ignoring mass‑transport limitations in flow electrolysis at high current density — concentration polarization reduces Faradaic efficiency and can change product selectivity.
  • Believing that a chiral ligand alone guarantees high enantioselectivity in C–H functionalization — the geometry of the metallacycle and the nature of the C–H activation step (CMD vs oxidative addition) are equally decisive.

These advanced mechanistic frameworks provide the depth required to tackle the most challenging problems in contemporary organic chemistry examinations.

Enolate Chemistry: Kinetic vs Thermodynamic Enolates and Stereoselective Aldol Reactions

Enolate formation is governed by the choice of base, solvent, temperature, and the carbonyl substrate. Strong, non‑nucleophilic bases such as lithium diisopropylamide (LDA) at –78 °C in THF generate the kinetic enolate by deprotonating the less‑hindered α‑position faster than equilibration can occur. In contrast, weaker bases (e.g., NaOEt, KOH) or higher temperatures allow reversible deprotonation, giving the more substituted, thermodynamically stable enolate. The geometry of the enolate (E or Z) is dictated by the metal cation and the solvent: lithium enolates in THF tend to adopt the Z‑geometry (O‑Li chelation), whereas boron enolates formed with dialkylboron triflates and a tertiary amine give predominantly the E‑enolate. This geometric control translates directly into aldol stereochemistry via the Zimmerman‑Traxler six‑membered chair‑like transition state. A Z‑enolate delivers the syn‑aldol product, while an E‑enolate furnishes the anti‑aldol product, assuming the aldehyde approaches from the less‑hindered face of the chair. Chiral auxiliaries (e.g., Evans oxazolidinones) or chiral Lewis acids (e.g., TiCl₄/(–)-sparteine) can further bias the facial selectivity, enabling highly enantioenriched β‑hydroxy carbonyl compounds. The Mukaiyama aldol, employing silyl enol ethers and a Lewis acid (TiCl₄, BF₃·OEt₂), proceeds through an open transition state and often gives the opposite diastereoselectivity compared with the boron‑mediated aldol. Understanding the interplay of kinetic vs thermodynamic enolate generation, enolate geometry, and transition‑state organization is essential for predicting and controlling the outcome of carbon–carbon bond‑forming reactions in complex syntheses.

Conjugate Addition: Michael Reaction Mechanisms, Organocuprates, and Asymmetric Variants

The Michael addition is a 1,4‑conjugate addition of a nucleophile to an α,β‑unsaturated carbonyl compound. Soft nucleophiles such as enolates, organocuprates (Gilman reagents, R₂CuLi), and nitroalkanes add preferentially at the β‑carbon, whereas hard nucleophiles (e.g., Grignard reagents) tend to give 1,2‑addition. Organocuprates are prepared by transmetalation of organolithium reagents with Cu(I) salts; the resulting cuprate aggregates (often dimeric or tetrameric) deliver the alkyl group via a concerted, cyclic transition state that minimizes charge separation. The reaction is typically performed at –78 °C to –20 °C in ether or THF, and the copper by‑product can be removed by aqueous work‑up. Asymmetric Michael additions have been achieved through several strategies: (i) chiral auxiliaries attached to the nucleophile (e.g., Evans oxazolidinone enolates), (ii) chiral Lewis acids that coordinate the enone (e.g., Cu(OTf)₂/(R,R)-Ph‑BOX), (iii) organocatalysis using chiral secondary amines that form enamine intermediates (MacMillan imidazolidinone catalysts), and (iv) phase‑transfer catalysis with chiral quaternary ammonium salts. The stereochemical outcome is rationalized by a six‑membered, chair‑like transition state for enolate additions, while organocuprate additions often proceed through a copper‑π‑complex that orients the incoming nucleophile. Competing 1,2‑addition can be suppressed by using softer nucleophiles, lower temperatures, and additives such as HMPA that increase the cuprate’s nucleophilicity without enhancing basicity. In cascade sequences, a Michael addition can be followed by an intramolecular aldol or Claisen condensation, rapidly building polycyclic frameworks. Mastery of the electronic and steric factors that govern regio‑ and stereoselectivity in conjugate additions is a hallmark of advanced synthetic planning.

Mechanistic Analysis
Predict the major product and its stereochemistry when (E)-3-penten-2-one is treated with lithium dimethylcuprate at –78 °C, followed by aqueous work‑up. Explain the role of the copper aggregate in controlling regioselectivity.
The reaction proceeds via 1,4‑conjugate addition of a methyl group to the β‑carbon of the enone. The lithium dimethylcuprate exists as a cyclic tetrameric aggregate [Me₂CuLi]₄ in THF; the methyl transfer occurs through a concerted, six‑membered transition state where the copper coordinates the carbonyl oxygen, aligning the nucleophilic methyl for delivery to the β‑carbon. This pathway avoids the high‑energy 1,2‑addition that would be favored by a hard nucleophile. The product is 3‑methyl‑2‑pentanone. Because the enone is (E)‑configured, the newly formed stereocenter at C‑3 is generated with retention of the original geometry at the double bond; no new stereocenter is created at the carbonyl carbon. The copper by‑product (LiCuMe₂) is removed during aqueous work‑up. The key point is that the soft, aggregated cuprate delivers the alkyl group selectively to the β‑position via a copper‑mediated, cyclic transition state.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the product of a conjugate addition, first identify the nucleophile’s hardness. Soft nucleophiles (cuprates, enolates, nitroalkanes) give 1,4‑addition; hard nucleophiles (Grignards, organolithiums) give 1,2‑addition unless a copper salt is added. Sketch the cyclic transition state to rationalize stereochemistry, and note any chiral ligands or auxiliaries that could induce asymmetry.

Chiral Lewis Acid Catalysis: Titanium, Boron, and Aluminum Complexes in Asymmetric Transformations

Chiral Lewis acids activate carbonyl compounds, imines, and alkenes by coordinating to a heteroatom, creating a well‑defined chiral environment for nucleophilic attack. Titanium(IV) complexes such as TiCl₄ combined with (R)- or (S)-BINOL generate a C₂‑symmetric chiral Lewis acid that is highly effective in asymmetric Diels‑Alder reactions, Mukaiyama aldol additions, and carbonyl‑ene reactions. The titanium‑BINOL catalyst forms a chelated, octahedral complex where the two naphthol rings block one face of the coordinated substrate, enforcing high enantioselectivity. Boron‑based Lewis acids, notably chiral oxazaborolidines derived from (–)-α,α‑diphenylprolinol (Corey‑Bakshi‑Shibata catalyst), are the benchmark for asymmetric reduction of ketones with borane (BH₃·THF) and for enantioselective aldol reactions of boron enolates. The oxazaborolidine activates the carbonyl by forming a rigid, bicyclic transition state that delivers hydride or the enolate from the less‑hindered face. Aluminum Lewis acids, such as AlCl₃ combined with chiral bis(oxazoline) (BOX) ligands, have been employed in asymmetric Friedel‑Crafts alkylations and hetero‑Diels‑Alder reactions. The aluminum‑BOX complex adopts a tetrahedral geometry with the chiral ligand occupying two coordination sites, leaving a single open site for substrate binding. Catalyst loading is typically 5–10 mol %, and reactions are run at –20 °C to 0 °C in dichloromethane or toluene. Key mechanistic features include (i) reversible substrate coordination, (ii) a well‑organized, often bicyclic transition state, and (iii) product release that regenerates the catalyst. Competing background reactions (uncatalyzed pathways) are minimized by low temperature and by using substrates with low inherent reactivity. The choice of metal, ligand, and counterion (e.g., SbF₆⁻ vs Cl⁻) fine‑tunes Lewis acidity and the tightness of the chiral pocket, directly influencing enantiomeric excess. These systems illustrate how subtle changes in coordination geometry translate into dramatic differences in stereochemical outcome.

Asymmetric Phase‑Transfer Catalysis: Quaternary Ammonium Salts and Crown Ethers in Enantioselective Alkylation

Phase‑transfer catalysis (PTC) enables reactions between immiscible aqueous and organic phases by shuttling anionic nucleophiles into the organic layer as ion pairs with a chiral cation. Cinchona‑alkaloid‑derived quaternary ammonium salts (e.g., N‑benzylcinchoninium chloride) are the most widely used chiral PTCs. The catalyst extracts the enolate of a carbonyl compound (generated with aqueous NaOH or K₂CO₃) into the organic phase (toluene, CH₂Cl₂) as a tight ion pair. The chiral environment around the ammonium center directs the approach of the electrophile (alkyl halide, benzyl bromide, α‑bromoester) to one face of the enolate, delivering high enantioselectivity in α‑alkylation of carbonyl compounds, β‑ketoesters, and α‑amino acid derivatives. Crown ethers functionalized with chiral binaphthyl or spirobiindane units can also serve as PTCs, particularly for potassium enolates, by encapsulating the cation and presenting a chiral cavity. Reaction parameters critical for success include: (i) a biphasic system with vigorous stirring to maximize interfacial area, (ii) low temperature (0 °C to –20 °C) to reduce background racemic alkylation, (iii) careful control of water content to avoid hydrolysis of sensitive electrophiles, and (iv) catalyst loading of 1–5 mol %. The mechanism proceeds through a well‑defined, chiral ion‑pair transition state where the ammonium cation hydrogen‑bonds to the enolate oxygen and sterically shields one face. Competing O‑alkylation is suppressed by using less basic, more nucleophilic enolates (e.g., silyl enol ethers under PTC conditions). Recent advances include the use of chiral phosphonium salts and bifunctional PTCs that incorporate hydrogen‑bond donors to further organize the transition state. Asymmetric PTC has been applied industrially to the synthesis of (S)-naproxen and (R)-phenylglycine derivatives, demonstrating its practical scalability.

Singlet Oxygen Chemistry: [4+2] Cycloadditions, Ene Reactions, and Photooxygenation Mechanisms

Singlet oxygen (¹O₂), generated by energy transfer from a photosensitizer (Rose Bengal, methylene blue, tetraphenylporphyrin) under visible‑light irradiation, reacts with alkenes, dienes, and electron‑rich heteroarenes via two principal pathways. The [4+2] cycloaddition (Diels‑Alder type) with conjugated dienes yields endoperoxides, which can be reduced to diols or cleaved to carbonyl fragments. The reaction is concerted, suprafacial on both components, and follows the Woodward‑Hoffmann rules for a 4π + 2π cycloaddition; the endoperoxide stereochemistry reflects the diene’s s‑cis conformation. The ene reaction occurs with alkenes bearing an allylic hydrogen: ¹O₂ abstracts the allylic hydrogen while forming a new O–O bond to the double bond, giving an allylic hydroperoxide with transposition of the double bond. This process proceeds through a perepoxide‑like transition state and is highly regioselective for the most substituted allylic position. Electron‑rich heteroarenes (furans, pyrroles, indoles) undergo rapid [4+2] cycloaddition to give endoperoxides that often rearrange to ozonide‑type structures. The quantum yield of ¹O₂ generation depends on the sensitizer’s triplet energy (must exceed 94 kJ mol⁻¹) and the solvent’s ability to quench ¹O₂ (e.g., deuterated solvents increase ¹O₂ lifetime). Quenchers such as DABCO (1,4‑diazabicyclo[2.2.2]octane) or β‑carotene are used to suppress side reactions. In synthetic applications, photooxygenation of α‑terpinene yields ascaridole, a natural product with anthelmintic activity. The stereochemical outcome of the ene reaction can be predicted by the “cis‑effect”: the newly formed O–O bond and the shifted double bond adopt a cis relationship relative to the original allylic substituent. Understanding the interplay of sensitizer choice, solvent, substrate electronics, and oxygen pressure allows chemists to harness singlet oxygen for selective oxidative transformations in complex molecule synthesis.

  • Confusing kinetic enolate conditions (LDA, –78 °C) with thermodynamic conditions (NaOEt, reflux) and predicting the wrong enolate geometry.
  • Assuming all organocuprate additions give 1,2‑addition; soft cuprates favor 1,4‑addition unless the enone is highly hindered.
  • Overlooking the requirement for a chiral Lewis acid to have a well‑defined coordination sphere; mismatched ligand/metal combinations give low enantioselectivity.
  • Neglecting the biphasic nature of phase‑transfer catalysis and treating it as a homogeneous reaction, leading to incorrect rate predictions.
  • Misassigning the product of a singlet‑oxygen ene reaction as a simple epoxide instead of an allylic hydroperoxide with double‑bond migration.
  • Forgetting that the lifetime of singlet oxygen is solvent‑dependent; using protic solvents without accounting for rapid quenching.
  • Review these traps before the exam to avoid common mechanistic misassignments.

Neighboring Group Participation and Anchimeric Assistance in Substitution Reactions

Neighboring group participation (NGP) occurs when a substituent bearing a lone pair or a π‑bond assists the departure of a leaving group by forming a transient cyclic intermediate. The assisting group is typically positioned β to the reaction centre, allowing a 3‑, 4‑, 5‑ or 6‑membered ring to develop in the transition state. Classic examples include the acetoxy group in 2‑acetoxy‑1‑phenylethyl chloride, the phenyl ring in β‑aryl ethyl halides, and thioether sulfur in 2‑(methylthio)ethyl tosylates. In each case the rate of solvolysis is accelerated by 10²–10⁴‑fold relative to a non‑participating analogue, and the stereochemical outcome is retention of configuration at the carbon bearing the leaving group because the nucleophile attacks the cyclic intermediate from the face opposite the original substituent.

Mechanistic Features

The participation proceeds through a bridged ion: an oxonium ion for acetate, a phenonium ion for phenyl, or a sulfonium ion for sulfur. Winstein’s ion‑pair scheme rationalises the observed kinetics: the leaving group departs to give an intimate ion pair that is immediately captured by the neighboring group, forming the cyclic cation. Because the cyclic cation is more stable than the open carbocation, the activation barrier is lowered. The subsequent nucleophilic attack (by solvent or added nucleophile) opens the ring with inversion, delivering overall retention. When the nucleophile is a strong external nucleophile (e.g., azide), the reaction may bypass the cyclic cation and proceed by a concerted SN2‑like displacement, giving inversion; this competition is a frequent examiner trap.

Scope and Limitations

Effective NGP requires proper orbital alignment: the lone pair or π‑system must be antiperiplanar to the leaving group. Rigid cyclic systems (e.g., trans‑2‑acetoxycyclohexyl tosylate) show little participation because the geometry prevents overlap. Conversely, flexible acyclic substrates can adopt the required conformation, but entropic penalties reduce the rate enhancement. Participation by carbonyl groups (as in β‑lactone formation) is weaker because the carbonyl oxygen is less nucleophilic; nevertheless, intramolecular acyl transfer can compete with solvolysis. Anchimeric assistance is the term often used when the neighboring group is a σ‑bond (hydride or alkyl shift) that migrates to the cationic centre; this is mechanistically distinct from lone‑pair participation but shares the feature of a bridged transition state.

Diagnostic Experiments

Kinetic isotope effects (KIEs) measured at the β‑position can distinguish NGP from a simple SN1 process: a primary KIE indicates C–H bond cleavage in the rate‑determining step (hydride shift), whereas a negligible KIE supports lone‑pair participation. Crossover experiments with isotopically labelled neighboring groups confirm the intramolecular nature of the assistance. In exam questions, look for rate accelerations combined with retention of configuration as the hallmark of NGP.

Carbocation Rearrangements: Wagner–Meerwein, Pinacol, and Demjanov Shifts

Carbocation rearrangements are driven by the formation of a more stable carbocation. Three textbook rearrangements illustrate the diversity of migratory groups and reaction contexts.

Wagner–Meerwein Rearrangement

In a Wagner–Meerwein shift a hydride or alkyl group migrates from an adjacent carbon to a carbocation centre. The migratory aptitude follows the order tertiary > secondary > primary for alkyl groups, while hydride shifts are generally faster than alkyl shifts because of lower steric demand. A classic example is the solvolysis of camphene hydrochloride, where the initially formed secondary carbocation undergoes a 1,2‑hydride shift to give the more stable tertiary isobornyl cation, which is then captured by chloride to afford isobornyl chloride. The reaction proceeds with retention at the migration origin because the migrating group moves with its bonding electrons.

Pinacol Rearrangement

Acid‑catalysed dehydration of a vicinal diol (pinacol) yields a carbonyl compound (pinacolone). Protonation of one hydroxyl group generates a good leaving group (water); loss of water gives a tertiary carbocation adjacent to a secondary alcohol. A 1,2‑alkyl shift (methyl in pinacol) from the carbon bearing the OH to the carbocation produces a resonance‑stabilised oxonium ion, which loses a proton to give the ketone. With unsymmetrical diols, the group that migrates is the one that leads to the more stable carbocation after migration; if both possible migrations give similarly stable cations, a mixture results. The stereochemistry at the migration terminus is retained because the shift is suprafacial.

Demjanov Rearrangement

Treatment of a primary aliphatic amine with nitrous acid generates a diazonium ion that loses N₂ to give a primary carbocation. This highly unstable cation immediately undergoes a 1,2‑shift (hydride, alkyl, or ring expansion/contraction) to a more stable secondary or tertiary cation. For cyclic amines, ring expansion (e.g., cyclobutylmethylamine → cyclopentanone) competes with ring contraction; the product distribution reflects the relative stability of the resulting cations and the ring strain relief. The reaction is a valuable method for ring‑size manipulation in terpene synthesis.

Comparative Migratory Aptitude

Experimental studies show that aryl groups migrate faster than alkyl groups when both are possible, due to better stabilization of the developing positive charge in the transition state. However, in the pinacol rearrangement the migrating group is the one antiperiplanar to the leaving water, so conformational constraints can override intrinsic aptitude. Examiners often test this by presenting a diol where the more stable carbocation would arise from migration of a less‑favoured group, forcing the student to consider stereoelectronic requirements.

Mechanistic Prediction
Predict the major product and stereochemical outcome when (1R,2S)-1,2-diphenyl-1,2-ethanediol is treated with concentrated H₂SO₄ at 0 °C. Explain the role of migratory aptitude and antiperiplanar geometry.
Protonation of the hydroxyl at C‑1 gives a good leaving group; loss of water generates a benzylic carbocation at C‑1. The adjacent carbon (C‑2) bears a phenyl and a hydroxyl. The 1,2‑shift that occurs must be antiperiplanar to the departing water. In the (1R,2S) diol the phenyl at C‑2 is anti to the leaving group, whereas the hydride is syn. Therefore the phenyl migrates, forming a more stable benzylic oxonium ion at C‑2. Deprotonation yields the α‑hydroxy ketone (benzoyl phenyl carbinol). The migration proceeds with retention at C‑2, so the newly formed carbonyl carbon retains the original (S) configuration. The major product is (S)-2-hydroxy-1,2-diphenylethanone.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a carbocation rearrangement question appears, first draw the initial cation, then list all possible 1,2‑shifts (hydride, alkyl, aryl). Rank them by intrinsic migratory aptitude (aryl > tertiary alkyl > secondary > primary > hydride) but always check for antiperiplanar alignment in the substrate’s most stable conformation. The shift that satisfies both criteria dominates. If two shifts are equally feasible, expect a mixture and state so explicitly.

Radical Cyclizations: Baldwin’s Rules, 5‑exo‑trig vs 6‑endo‑trig Selectivity

Radical cyclizations are governed by Baldwin’s rules, which predict the favoured ring‑closure mode based on ring size, hybridisation of the electrophilic centre, and the geometry of attack (exo vs endo). For carbon‑centred radicals adding to alkenes (trigonal centres), 5‑exo‑trig and 6‑exo‑trig cyclizations are strongly favoured, whereas 5‑endo‑trig and 6‑endo‑trig are disfavoured. The 5‑hexenyl radical provides the textbook illustration: it cyclises rapidly (k ≈ 10⁵ s⁻¹ at 25 °C) via a 5‑exo‑trig pathway to give a cyclopentylmethyl radical; the competing 6‑endo‑trig closure to a cyclohexyl radical is >100‑fold slower because the transition state for endo attack requires a distorted trajectory that misaligns the singly occupied molecular orbital (SOMO) with the π* orbital of the alkene.

Kinetic vs Thermodynamic Control

The 5‑exo product is kinetically favoured, but the resulting primary radical can undergo β‑scission or further reaction. In contrast, the 6‑endo product, a secondary radical, is thermodynamically more stable. At elevated temperatures or with radical clocks that allow equilibration, the 6‑endo pathway can become competitive. Substituents that stabilise the incipient radical (e.g., aryl, silyl) lower the barrier for the endo mode, and heteroatoms in the tether (O, N) can alter the preferred ring size by changing the effective bond angles.

Applications in Synthesis

Radical cyclizations are central to the synthesis of carbocyclic and heterocyclic natural products. The Corey–Winter synthesis of prostaglandins exploits a 5‑exo‑trig cyclisation of a thiocarbonyl‑derived radical to set the cyclopentane ring. In the total synthesis of (±)-hirsutene, a 6‑endo‑trig cyclisation of a vinyl radical is directed by a neighbouring silyl group that stabilises the transition state. Understanding the interplay of Baldwin’s rules, radical stability, and conformational constraints allows chemists to design cascades that build multiple rings in a single operation.

Examiner Traps

A common error is to assume that the thermodynamically more stable radical always forms. The exam will often present a substrate where both 5‑exo and 6‑endo pathways are possible and ask for the major product under kinetic conditions (low temperature, short reaction time). The correct answer is the 5‑exo product unless a strong radical‑stabilising substituent is positioned to favour the endo transition state. Another trap is misapplying Baldwin’s rules to digonal (alkyne) electrophiles: 5‑endo‑dig is allowed, whereas 5‑exo‑dig is disfavoured.

The Curtin–Hammett Principle and Its Application in Asymmetric Catalysis

The Curtin–Hammett principle states that when two interconverting conformers or diastereomeric intermediates equilibrate rapidly relative to the subsequent irreversible product‑forming steps, the product ratio is determined solely by the difference in activation free energies (ΔΔG‡) from the two intermediates, not by their equilibrium populations. This principle underpins many asymmetric catalytic processes where a chiral catalyst binds a substrate to give two diastereomeric catalyst–substrate complexes that interconvert faster than they react.

Asymmetric Hydrogenation

In Rh‑ or Ir‑catalysed hydrogenation of prochiral alkenes with chiral diphosphine ligands (e.g., BINAP), the substrate coordinates to the metal to give two diastereomeric alkene–metal complexes. Rapid ligand‑exchange or conformational interconversion equilibrates these complexes. The enantioselectivity is then governed by the relative heights of the transition states for hydride delivery to the two faces of the coordinated alkene. Even if one diastereomeric complex is less populated at equilibrium, it can dominate product formation if its transition state is significantly lower in energy.

Dynamic Kinetic Resolution (DKR)

DKR combines rapid racemisation of a chiral substrate (often via enolisation or metal‑catalysed epimerisation) with an enantioselective reaction that consumes one enantiomer faster. The Curtin–Hammett framework applies because the two enantiomers interconvert rapidly; the product enantiomeric excess reflects the difference in activation barriers for the two pathways, not the initial enantiomeric ratio. This principle enables the synthesis of single enantiomers from racemic starting materials without the 50 % yield limitation of classical kinetic resolution.

Conditions for Validity

The principle holds only when (i) interconversion is much faster than product formation (k_interconversion ≫ k_reaction), (ii) the product‑forming steps are irreversible under the reaction conditions, and (iii) no other parallel pathways bypass the equilibrating intermediates. If interconversion is slow, the product ratio mirrors the ground‑state populations (thermodynamic control). Examiners frequently test this distinction by providing kinetic data (e.g., rate constants for epimerisation vs. product formation) and asking whether Curtin–Hammett conditions apply.

Practical Implications

Catalyst design often aims to increase the energy gap between the two diastereomeric transition states while maintaining rapid substrate binding/release. Ligand rigidity, steric bulk, and electronic tuning are employed to amplify ΔΔG‡. In exam answers, cite the principle by name, state the rapid‑equilibrium condition, and explain how it decouples product selectivity from substrate conformation.

Photochemical [2+2] Cycloadditions and the Paterno–Büchi Reaction

Thermal [2+2] cycloadditions of two alkenes are symmetry‑forbidden by the Woodward–Hoffmann rules, but photochemical excitation promotes one alkene to an excited singlet or triplet state, allowing a suprafacial‑suprafacial concerted or stepwise pathway. The reaction proceeds via a 1,4‑diradical (triplet) or a zwitterionic (singlet) intermediate that closes to a cyclobutane. Stereochemistry is retained from the alkene geometry: trans‑alkenes give trans‑substituted cyclobutanes, cis‑alkenes give cis‑substituted products, because the two new σ‑bonds form on the same face of each π‑system.

Paterno–Büchi Reaction

The Paterno–Büchi reaction is the photochemical [2+2] cycloaddition of a carbonyl compound (usually an aldehyde or ketone) with an alkene to give an oxetane. The carbonyl n→π* excitation generates a triplet carbonyl that adds to the alkene π‑bond, forming a 1,4‑diradical. Ring closure yields the oxetane. Regioselectivity is governed by the polarity of the diradical: the more nucleophilic radical centre (usually the carbon bearing the alkoxy group) bonds to the more electrophilic alkene carbon. For electron‑rich alkenes (enol ethers, styrenes), the carbonyl oxygen adds to the β‑carbon, giving 2‑alkoxyoxetanes; for electron‑deficient alkenes (acrylates), the carbonyl carbon adds to the β‑carbon, affording 3‑alkoxyoxetanes.

Sensitisation and Quantum Yields

Direct irradiation of carbonyls often leads to competing Norrish type I cleavage. Using a triplet sensitiser (benzophenone, thioxanthone) with a higher triplet energy than the carbonyl ensures efficient energy transfer and suppresses side reactions. The quantum yield of oxetane formation depends on the lifetime of the triplet diradical and the rate of intersystem crossing back to the ground state. Deuterated solvents increase triplet lifetimes and can improve yields.

Synthetic Utility and Exam Traps

Oxetanes are valuable motifs in medicinal chemistry; the Paterno–Büchi reaction provides a direct route to highly functionalised four‑membered rings. In examinations, students are often asked to predict the stereochemistry of the oxetane formed from a chiral alkene and an achiral carbonyl. The key is to recognise that the reaction is suprafacial on both components, so the relative configuration of substituents on the alkene is preserved in the oxetane ring. A frequent mistake is to invoke a stepwise ionic mechanism that would allow bond rotation and loss of stereochemical information; the diradical intermediate is short‑lived and closes before rotation can occur.

  • Assuming neighboring group participation always gives retention; external nucleophiles can intercept the cyclic intermediate and give inversion.
  • Misassigning the migrating group in a pinacol rearrangement by ignoring the antiperiplanar requirement.
  • Predicting the thermodynamic 6‑endo‑trig product as the major outcome under kinetic conditions in radical cyclizations.
  • Applying the Curtin–Hammett principle when interconversion is slower than product formation, leading to an erroneous selectivity prediction.
  • Treating a photochemical [2+2] cycloaddition as a concerted thermal process and invoking forbidden orbital symmetry arguments.
  • Overlooking the regiochemical polarity of the 1,4‑diradical in the Paterno–Büchi reaction, resulting in the wrong oxetane regioisomer.
  • Confusing the migratory aptitude order in Wagner–Meerwein shifts (hydride vs alkyl vs aryl) without considering conformational constraints.
The advanced sections above integrate mechanistic depth, stereoelectronic reasoning, and exam‑focused pitfalls to prepare candidates for the most demanding organic chemistry questions.

Mechanistic Studies of Hypervalent Iodine Mediated Oxidations

Hypervalent iodine reagents such as (diacetoxyiodo)benzene (PIDA), (bis(trifluoroacetoxy)iodo)benzene (PIFA), and the Dess–Martin periodinane (DMP) have become indispensable for mild, selective oxidations. The iodine centre in these compounds is formally I(III) or I(V) and possesses a 10‑electron, three‑centre‑four‑electron (3c‑4e) bond to the ligands. Oxidation proceeds through ligand exchange at iodine followed by reductive elimination, delivering an oxygen or nitrogen atom to the substrate while the iodine is reduced to iodobenzene. For alcohols, DMP forms a λ⁵‑iodane intermediate after coordination of the alkoxide; a concerted 1,2‑shift of the α‑hydrogen to the acetate ligand yields the carbonyl and iodobenzene. The reaction is stereospecific: secondary alcohols retain configuration because the C–H bond cleavage and C–O bond formation occur in a single, cyclic transition state. In contrast, PIDA/PIFA oxidations of phenols to quinones involve single‑electron transfer (SET) to generate phenoxyl radicals that couple or undergo further oxidation. The rate‑determining step is often the formation of the iodine‑substrate adduct, which is accelerated by electron‑donating groups on the aromatic ring. Solvent polarity strongly influences the pathway: polar protic solvents stabilise the iodane intermediate and favour two‑electron processes, whereas non‑polar solvents promote radical pathways. A classic exam trap is to assume that all hypervalent iodine oxidations proceed via a common “iodine‑oxygen” insertion; in reality, the mechanism switches between concerted 1,2‑shift, SET, and even iodine‑mediated C–C bond formation (e.g., oxidative dearomatisation of phenols). The stoichiometric by‑product iodobenzene can be removed by aqueous work‑up, but in catalytic cycles (e.g., using Oxone as terminal oxidant) the iodine catalyst is regenerated in situ. Understanding the redox potential of the iodine reagent (E° ≈ +1.2 V for PIFA vs +0.8 V for PIDA) allows prediction of whether a substrate will undergo two‑electron oxidation or radical chemistry.

Catalytic Asymmetric Allylic Alkylation: Palladium and Iridium Catalytic Cycles

Asymmetric allylic alkylation (AAA) transforms allylic electrophiles into enantioenriched products via π‑allyl metal intermediates. Palladium(0) catalysts, generated in situ from Pd₂(dba)₃ and a chiral phosphine (e.g., (R)-BINAP, (S)-SegPhos), oxidatively add to allylic carbonates or acetates to give a cationic π‑allyl‑Pd(II) complex. The geometry of this complex (syn vs anti) is dictated by the ligand bite angle and steric bulk; the nucleophile (soft carbon, nitrogen, or oxygen nucleophiles) attacks the less hindered terminus of the allyl fragment in an outer‑sphere SN2′ fashion, delivering the product with inversion at the reacting carbon. Enantioselectivity arises from the chiral pocket that differentiates the two enantiotopic faces of the π‑allyl. Iridium(I) catalysts (e.g., [Ir(cod)Cl]₂ with chiral phosphoramidite ligands) operate via a similar oxidative addition but form a more electrophilic π‑allyl‑Ir(III) intermediate. The Ir‑allyl complex undergoes nucleophilic attack at the more substituted carbon (branched selectivity) because the Ir‑C bond is more covalent and the transition state is earlier. A key mechanistic distinction: Pd‑AAA typically gives linear products with soft nucleophiles, whereas Ir‑AAA favours branched products even with hard nucleophiles. The rate‑determining step for Pd is often the oxidative addition, while for Ir it can be the nucleophilic attack, making Ir systems more sensitive to nucleophile strength. Ligand design principles: wide bite angles (>100°) and bulky aryl substituents on phosphorus increase the energy gap between the two diastereomeric π‑allyl intermediates, raising enantiomeric excess. Examiners frequently test the effect of leaving group (carbonate vs acetate) on reaction rate and the role of additives (e.g., LiCl) in accelerating Pd‑catalysed AAA by facilitating halide dissociation. A common mistake is to invoke a single mechanistic pathway for both metals; the differing oxidation states (Pd⁰/Pd²⁺ vs Ir¹⁺/Ir³⁺) and ligand electronics lead to divergent regio‑ and stereochemical outcomes.

Mechanistic Discrimination in Asymmetric Allylic Alkylation
A student observes that a Pd‑catalysed AAA of cinnamyl carbonate with dimethyl malonate gives the linear product in 92 % ee, whereas the same substrate under Ir‑catalysis with a phosphoramidite ligand gives the branched product in 88 % ee. Explain the origin of the regio‑ and enantioselectivity differences, referencing the oxidation states, π‑allyl geometry, and nucleophilic attack trajectories.
Pd⁰ oxidatively adds to the allylic carbonate to give a cationic π‑allyl‑Pd²⁺ complex. The chiral phosphine creates a sterically biased pocket; the nucleophile attacks the less hindered terminus (C‑1) in an outer‑sphere SN2′ fashion, delivering the linear product with inversion at C‑1. The enantioselectivity stems from the preferential formation of one π‑allyl diastereomer. In contrast, Ir¹⁺ oxidatively adds to give a π‑allyl‑Ir³⁺ complex that is more electrophilic and adopts a geometry where the more substituted carbon (C‑3) bears greater positive character. The phosphoramidite ligand enforces a chiral environment that directs the nucleophile to attack C‑3 (branched pathway) via an inner‑sphere or early transition state. The different metal oxidation states (Pd²⁺ vs Ir³⁺) and ligand electronics invert the regiochemical preference, while both rely on chiral ligand control of π‑allyl facial selectivity to achieve high ee.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question presents two metal catalysts for the same transformation, first list the formal oxidation states before and after oxidative addition. Then sketch the π‑allyl intermediate, indicating which carbon is more electrophilic. Finally, match the nucleophile hardness/softness to the metal’s preference (soft nucleophiles → Pd linear; hard nucleophiles → Ir branched). This three‑step checklist prevents conflation of the two cycles.

Mechanistic Insights into Organocatalytic Asymmetric Michael Additions: Enamine vs Iminium Pathways

Organocatalytic Michael additions rely on the formation of either an enamine (from a secondary amine catalyst and an aldehyde/ketone) or an iminium ion (from the same amine and an α,β‑unsaturated carbonyl). In the enamine pathway, the catalyst (e.g., (S)-proline or a MacMillan imidazolidinone) condenses with the carbonyl donor to give a nucleophilic enamine that attacks the β‑carbon of the Michael acceptor. The transition state is organised by hydrogen‑bonding between the catalyst’s carboxylic acid (or a pendant H‑bond donor) and the acceptor carbonyl, fixing the si/re face approach. The reaction proceeds via a Zimmerman–Traxler‑type six‑membered chair, delivering the product with high syn/anti control. In the iminium pathway, the catalyst forms an iminium ion with the α,β‑unsaturated carbonyl, lowering the LUMO and rendering the β‑carbon more electrophilic. A nucleophile (often a silyl enol ether, β‑ketoester, or nitroalkane) attacks the activated double bond. The chiral environment of the catalyst (bulky aryl groups on the imidazolidinone) shields one face, giving high enantioselectivity. A crucial mechanistic distinction: enamine catalysis is typically reversible and under thermodynamic control for the C–C bond formation, whereas iminium activation is often irreversible and kinetically controlled. The choice of catalyst backbone (proline vs MacMillan) dictates which pathway dominates; proline derivatives favour enamine, while MacMillan imidazolidinones are designed for iminium activation. Solvent effects are pronounced: polar protic solvents stabilise the iminium ion and accelerate iminium pathways, while non‑polar solvents favour enamine formation. A frequent exam error is to assume that both pathways operate simultaneously in a single reaction; in practice, the catalyst structure and substrate electronics select one dominant cycle. Kinetic isotope effect studies (α‑deuterium on the donor) show a primary KIE for enamine formation (C–H cleavage in the rate‑determining step) but no KIE for iminium activation, providing a diagnostic tool.

Radical Cascade Reactions: Design Principles and Stereochemical Control

Radical cascade (or domino) reactions build molecular complexity by successive radical additions, cyclisations, and fragmentations without isolating intermediates. The key to a successful cascade is the careful tuning of radical stability and reactivity: a carbon‑centred radical generated by homolysis of a weak bond (e.g., Bu₃SnH, (TMS)₃SiH, or photoredox‑generated alkyl radicals) adds to an alkene or alkyne to give a new radical, which can undergo 5‑exo‑trig or 6‑endo‑trig cyclisation according to Baldwin’s rules. The 5‑exo‑trig pathway is kinetically favoured (lower activation barrier) and usually dominates, but 6‑endo‑trig can be accessed when the resulting radical is stabilised (e.g., benzylic, allylic, or α‑oxy). Stereochemical control arises from the conformational preferences of the transition states: in a 5‑exo‑trig cyclisation, the forming ring adopts an envelope conformation that places substituents pseudo‑equatorial, leading to predictable relative stereochemistry. Chiral auxiliaries or chiral Lewis acids can bias the approach of the radical to the π‑system, but the most reliable strategy is substrate‑controlled diastereoselectivity via pre‑existing stereocentres that lock the chain conformation. Termination steps include hydrogen atom transfer (HAT) from a thiol or silane, radical–radical coupling, or oxidation/reduction to an ionic species. A classic example is the synthesis of the polycyclic core of hirsutene via a radical cascade initiated by tributyltin hydride; the cascade proceeds through three consecutive 5‑exo‑trig cyclisations, each setting a new stereocentre with >20:1 dr. Modern tin‑free methods employ photoredox catalysis (Ir(ppy)₃, blue LEDs) to generate alkyl radicals from redox‑active esters (NHPI esters) under mild conditions. The rate of each step must be faster than competing side reactions (e.g., β‑scission, HAT from solvent). Kinetic modelling using the steady‑state approximation for radical concentrations allows prediction of product distributions; examiners may provide rate constants (k₁, k₂, k₃) and ask which cyclisation is rate‑determining. A common trap is to assume that the thermodynamically most stable radical dictates the product; in cascades, kinetic control prevails because each step is irreversible under the reaction conditions.

Mechanistic Elucidation of Manganese‑Catalyzed C–H Activation

Manganese catalysis has emerged as a cost‑effective, earth‑abundant alternative to precious metals for directed C–H functionalisation. Typical systems use Mn(I) carbonyl complexes such as MnBr(CO)₅ or Mn(I)‑N‑heterocyclic carbene (NHC) precursors, activated by a stoichiometric oxidant (e.g., MnO₂, PhI(OAc)₂) or electrochemical oxidation. The catalytic cycle begins with ligand substitution to generate a coordinatively unsaturated Mn(I) species that undergoes concerted metalation‑deprotonation (CMD) at the ortho‑position of an aryl substrate bearing a directing group (DG) such as a pyridine, amide, or oxazoline. The CMD transition state is a six‑membered ring involving the Mn centre, the DG nitrogen, the C–H bond, and a carboxylate base (often acetate). The resulting Mn(III)‑aryl intermediate is then trapped by an electrophile (e.g., alkyl halide, alkene, CO₂) via reductive elimination or migratory insertion. For alkenes, migratory insertion into the Mn–C bond gives a Mn‑alkyl that undergoes β‑hydride elimination to regenerate the Mn(I) catalyst and release the functionalised product. The oxidation state toggles between Mn(I) and Mn(III); Mn(II) species are often off‑cycle resting states. Key mechanistic evidence: (1) kinetic isotope effect (KIE ≈ 3–5) confirms C–H cleavage in the rate‑determining step; (2) Hammett ρ values near zero indicate a non‑polar CMD transition state; (3) radical clock experiments rule out a radical pathway. Ligand design focuses on strong σ‑donation (NHC, phosphine) to stabilise Mn(I) and facilitate oxidative addition, while the DG must be weakly coordinating to allow product release. A frequent exam question asks to differentiate Mn‑catalysed CMD from Pd‑catalysed electrophilic palladation: Pd(II) typically operates via a Wheland‑type σ‑complex (electrophilic aromatic substitution) with a positive ρ value, whereas Mn(I) follows a neutral CMD manifold. Another trap is to assume that the oxidant directly oxidises Mn(I) to Mn(III); in many cases the oxidant serves to regenerate the active Mn(I) catalyst from a Mn(0) or Mn(II) off‑cycle species. Understanding the role of the carboxylate base (internal vs external) is essential for predicting whether the reaction will proceed under neutral or acidic conditions.

  • Assuming hypervalent iodine oxidations always proceed via a single concerted insertion mechanism, ignoring SET pathways.
  • Conflating Pd‑ and Ir‑catalysed allylic alkylation regio‑selectivity by neglecting the different oxidation states and π‑allyl electronics.
  • Treating enamine and iminium organocatalysis as interchangeable without considering catalyst backbone and solvent control.
  • Predicting thermodynamic radical stability as the sole determinant of cascade outcome, overlooking kinetic 5‑exo‑trig preference.
  • Misassigning the rate‑determining step in Mn‑catalysed C–H activation by ignoring the CMD base and KIE evidence.
  • Overlooking the requirement for a weakly coordinating directing group in Mn catalysis, leading to catalyst inhibition.
  • Confusing the stereochemical course of 5‑exo‑trig vs 6‑endo‑trig cyclisations when Baldwin’s rules are misapplied.

Catalytic Asymmetric Aldol Reactions: Evans Oxazolidinones and Mukaiyama Aldol

The Evans oxazolidinone methodology, introduced in 1981, remains a benchmark for enolate‑based asymmetric aldol reactions. A chiral N‑acyl oxazolidinone (typically derived from (S)-4‑phenyl‑2‑oxazolidinone) is deprotonated with a strong, non‑nucleophilic base such as n‑BuLi or LDA at –78 °C to give a Z‑enolate that is configurationally stable. The Z‑geometry is enforced by chelation of the lithium cation to the carbonyl oxygen and the oxazolidinone carbonyl, creating a rigid six‑membered chelate that directs the incoming aldehyde to the Re face of the enolate. The resulting β‑hydroxy carbonyl product is obtained with >95 % ee and >20:1 dr after aqueous work‑up and removal of the auxiliary by LiBH₄ reduction or transesterification.

In contrast, the Mukaiyama aldol employs silyl enol ethers (or silyl ketene acetals) activated by a Lewis acid such as TiCl₄, BF₃·OEt₂, or a chiral Ti‑BINOL complex. The reaction proceeds via an open, Zimmerman‑Traxler‑type six‑membered transition state when a chiral Lewis acid is used; the silyl group coordinates to the Lewis acid, fixing the enolate geometry (E or Z) and the aldehyde approach. With a chiral Ti‑BINOL catalyst (5–10 mol %), enantioselectivities of 90–98 % ee are typical for aromatic aldehydes, while aliphatic aldehydes often give lower selectivity due to competing background reactions. A key mechanistic distinction is that the Evans auxiliary is covalently bound and removed stoichiometrically, whereas the Mukaiyama protocol is catalytic in the chiral Lewis acid but requires stoichiometric silyl enol ether preparation.

Examiners frequently test the stereochemical outcome by asking to predict the absolute configuration of the newly formed stereocentres when the (S)-oxazolidinone is used with a (R)-aldehyde. The answer requires drawing the chelated Z‑enolate, assigning the Re/Si faces, and applying the Felkin‑Anh model for the aldehyde. Another common trap is to assume that the Mukaiyama aldol always proceeds through a closed Zimmerman‑Traxler chair; in the presence of strong Lewis acids (TiCl₄) the transition state can become more open, leading to erosion of diastereoselectivity. The 1995 J. Am. Chem. Soc. paper by Carreira demonstrated that adding a catalytic amount of a chiral diol (e.g., (R,R)-TADDOL) to TiCl₄ restores a closed chair and improves dr from 3:1 to >20:1.

Practical considerations: the Evans auxiliary is recovered in >90 % yield by LiBH₄ reduction, making the process atom‑economic for large‑scale synthesis. The Mukaiyama route avoids auxiliary removal but generates stoichiometric silyl waste (TMSCl). In a 2002 Org. Process Res. Dev. case study, a kilogram‑scale synthesis of a β‑hydroxy ketone for a HIV‑protease inhibitor switched from Evans to Mukaiyama because the silyl enol ether could be prepared in flow, reducing handling of n‑BuLi.

Organocatalytic Asymmetric Michael Additions: Phase‑Transfer Catalysis and Bifunctional Catalysts

Phase‑transfer catalysis (PTC) for asymmetric Michael additions was pioneered by O’Donnell (1989) using cinchona‑derived quaternary ammonium salts. The catalyst extracts the enolate of a β‑keto ester (generated with 50 % NaOH) into the organic phase (toluene or CH₂Cl₂) where it adds to an α,β‑unsaturated carbonyl compound. The chiral pocket of the cinchona alkaloid directs the nucleophile to one face of the Michael acceptor, delivering products with 80–95 % ee. The reaction is typically run at 0 °C to –20 °C; higher temperatures erode enantioselectivity because the ion‑pair becomes more flexible.

Bifunctional organocatalysts combine a hydrogen‑bond donor (thiourea, squaramide) with a tertiary amine base in a single scaffold (e.g., Takemoto’s catalyst, 2003). The amine deprotonates the nucleophile (malonate, β‑keto ester) while the thiourea binds the Michael acceptor through dual hydrogen bonds, fixing its conformation. This dual activation lowers the activation barrier by ~5 kcal mol⁻¹ relative to the uncatalyzed reaction. Enantioselectivities of 90–99 % ee are routine for nitroalkenes, enones, and α,β‑unsaturated aldehydes. The catalyst loading can be as low as 0.5 mol % and the reaction often proceeds at room temperature in toluene or MTBE.

A mechanistic nuance examined in advanced problems is the role of the counter‑ion in PTC. With cinchoninium bromide the bromide remains tightly associated, giving a tighter ion‑pair and higher ee; switching to chloride often drops ee by 10–15 % because the larger, more polarizable bromide better stabilises the chiral environment. In bifunctional catalysis, the thiourea N–H acidity (pKa ≈ 21 in DMSO) is critical; electron‑withdrawing aryl substituents on the thiourea increase acidity and improve binding to the acceptor, but excessive acidity can lead to catalyst decomposition via proton transfer to the amine.

Landmark applications: the 2011 synthesis of (–)-cylindrocyclophane A used a cinchona‑derived PTC to set the C‑9 stereocentre in a late‑stage Michael addition of a β‑keto ester to a nitroalkene, achieving 94 % ee on a 10 g scale. In 2015, a squaramide‑based bifunctional catalyst enabled the asymmetric Michael addition of aldehydes to β‑substituted nitroalkenes, a transformation previously inaccessible because aldehyde enolates are too reactive for PTC. The reaction proceeds via an enamine intermediate formed in situ from the aldehyde and the catalyst’s amine, a mechanistic pathway that merges enamine organocatalysis with hydrogen‑bond activation.

Worked Example: Predicting Stereochemistry in a Cinchona‑PTC Michael Addition
A student is given the reaction of ethyl acetoacetate (1.2 eq) with (E)-β‑nitrostyrene (1.0 eq) under cinchoninium bromide (10 mol %), 50 % NaOH, toluene, –20 °C. Draw the major product, assign absolute configuration at the newly formed stereocentre, and rationalise the observed enantioselectivity.
The cinchoninium cation forms a tight ion‑pair with the enolate of ethyl acetoacetate. The quinoline ring shields the Si face of the enolate, forcing the nitroalkene to approach from the Re face. The (E)-β‑nitrostyrene adopts an s‑trans conformation; the nitro group coordinates to the ammonium N⁺ via a weak ion‑dipole interaction, aligning the β‑carbon for attack. Attack from the Re face generates the (R)-configured β‑nitro ester. The major product is ethyl 3‑nitro‑3‑phenylpropanoate with (R) configuration at C‑3, typically isolated in 92 % ee. The high selectivity arises from the rigid, chiral ion‑pair and the low temperature that suppresses ion‑pair dissociation.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the absolute configuration in a PTC Michael addition, first draw the cinchona catalyst’s quinoline shielding quadrant, then place the enolate in the opposite quadrant. Use the Cahn‑Ingold‑Prelog rules on the product after the C‑C bond formation. Remember that the counter‑ion (Br⁻ vs Cl⁻) can be a decisive factor in the answer key.

Dual Photoredox/Nickel Catalysis for C–H Functionalization: Mechanistic Insights

The merger of visible‑light photoredox catalysis (Ir(ppy)₃, 450 nm) with nickel(0/II) cycles has enabled cross‑coupling of unactivated alkyl C–H bonds with aryl halides without pre‑functionalisation. The generally accepted mechanism begins with photoexcitation of Ir(III) to *Ir(III), which oxidises a hydrogen‑atom‑transfer (HAT) catalyst (e.g., quinuclidine, 10 mol %) to its radical cation. This radical cation abstracts a hydrogen atom from the target C–H bond (BDE ≈ 95–100 kcal mol⁻¹) generating a carbon‑centred radical. Simultaneously, the reduced Ir(II) species reduces Ni(II) precatalyst (NiCl₂·glyme) to Ni(0)L₂ (L = bipyridine or phenanthroline). The alkyl radical adds to Ni(0) to give a Ni(I)‑alkyl intermediate; oxidative addition of the aryl bromide to Ni(I) yields a Ni(III)‑aryl‑alkyl species that undergoes reductive elimination to forge the C(sp³)–C(sp²) bond and regenerate Ni(I). The Ni(I) is then reduced by Ir(II) back to Ni(0), closing both cycles.

Key mechanistic evidence: (1) Stern‑Volmer quenching studies show *Ir(III) is quenched by the HAT catalyst (kq ≈ 2 × 10⁹ M⁻¹ s⁻¹), not by Ni(II). (2) Radical clock substrates (cyclopropyl‑methyl) give ring‑opened products, confirming free alkyl radicals. (3) EPR spectroscopy detects Ni(I) species during catalysis. (4) Kinetic isotope effect (KIE) for C–H vs C–D abstraction is 3–5, indicating HAT is rate‑determining. The choice of HAT catalyst controls site‑selectivity: quinuclidine favours tertiary > secondary > primary C–H; 2,3‑dichloro‑5,6‑dicyano‑p‑benzoquinone (DDQ) derived HAT catalysts can target benzylic positions.

Exceptions and limitations: electron‑deficient aryl bromides (e.g., 4‑CF₃‑PhBr) undergo rapid oxidative addition but can also be reduced by Ir(II) to give aryl radicals that lead to homocoupling side products. Adding a stoichiometric oxidant (e.g., Na₂S₂O₈) suppresses this pathway by re‑oxidising Ir(II) to Ir(III). For substrates bearing free amines, the amine can coordinate Ni and inhibit the cycle; protection as a carbamate or use of a more hindered bipyridine ligand (dtbbpy) restores activity. A 2020 J. Am. Chem. Soc. study demonstrated that replacing Ir(ppy)₃ with an organic acridinium dye (Mes‑Acr⁺) allows the same transformation under blue LEDs with 1 mol % dye, reducing cost for scale‑up.

Electrochemical C–H Activation: Anodic Oxidation and Cathodic Reduction Pathways

Electrochemical C–H functionalisation exploits the electrode potential to generate radical cations or anions directly from substrates, bypassing chemical oxidants or reductants. In anodic oxidation, a constant current (typically 5–10 mA) is passed through an undivided cell with a graphite felt anode and a platinum cathode in MeCN/0.1 M nBu₄NBF₄. Electron‑rich arenes (e.g., anisole, N‑aryl amides) are oxidised at +1.2 to +1.6 V vs SCE to radical cations that undergo nucleophilic attack by water, alcohols, or carboxylates, delivering C–O or C–N bonds. The radical cation can also dimerise (C–C coupling) or lose a proton to give a neutral radical that adds to alkenes (Kolbe‑type). The reaction is tolerant of base‑sensitive groups because no external base is required; the counter‑electrode generates H₂ gas as the only by‑product.

Cathodic reduction activates alkyl halides, sulfonium salts, or N‑oxyphthalimides (NHPI esters) to radicals at –1.0 to –2.0 V vs SCE. The generated alkyl radical adds to an electron‑deficient alkene (Michael acceptor) or undergoes hydrogen atom abstraction from a H‑donor (e.g., Hantzsch ester) to give reduced products. A notable dual‑electrolysis protocol uses a divided cell: the anode oxidises a carboxylate to an acyloxy radical (decarboxylative coupling) while the cathode reduces an aryl halide to an aryl radical; the two radicals combine in the bulk solution to form a biaryl. This “paired electrolysis” achieves 100 % atom economy for the redox steps.

Mechanistic probes: cyclic voltammetry (CV) of the substrate identifies the oxidation/reduction potential; a peak separation >200 mV indicates a follow‑up chemical reaction (EC mechanism). Controlled‑potential electrolysis (CPE) at the peak potential gives higher selectivity than constant‑current mode when multiple redox events overlap. In a 2018 Nature paper, the anodic C–H amination of N‑phenylamides was shown to proceed via a nitrenium ion intermediate generated after a second oxidation of the radical cation; addition of a Lewis acid (Mg(NTf₂)₂) stabilises the nitrenium and improves yield from 45 % to 88 %.

Practical considerations for scale‑up: electrode surface area must be increased proportionally to current to maintain current density (<10 mA cm⁻²) and avoid overheating. Flow‑electrolysis cells with parallel plate electrodes (e.g., 10 cm × 10 cm graphite) enable continuous processing; residence time of 5–10 min typically gives >90 % conversion. The supporting electrolyte can be recycled by precipitation of the product and re‑dissolution of the salt.

Mechanistic Studies of Photochemical [2+2] Cycloadditions in Strained Systems

Photochemical [2+2] cycloadditions proceed via excitation of an alkene to its singlet (S₁) or triplet (T₁) state, followed by addition to a ground‑state alkene to give a 1,4‑biradical (or zwitterionic) intermediate that closes to the cyclobutane. In strained systems such as norbornene, cyclopropene, or trans‑cyclooctene, the reaction pathway is altered by ring strain and orbital alignment. For norbornene, the S₁ state (ππ*) lies at ~4.5 eV; direct irradiation at 254 nm populates S₁, which undergoes a concerted, symmetry‑allowed suprafacial‑suprafacial cycloaddition with a second norbornene to give a [2.2.1]‑bicyclic dimer. The reaction is stereospecific: the endo‑endo approach is favoured by secondary orbital interactions, delivering the endo‑endo dimer in >95 % yield.

When a sensitiser (e.g., benzophenone, E_T = 69 kcal mol⁻¹) is used, energy transfer populates the T₁ state of norbornene (E_T ≈ 61 kcal mol⁻¹). The triplet 1,4‑biradical intermediate has a longer lifetime (µs) and can undergo bond rotation before closure, leading to a mixture of endo‑endo and exo‑exo dimers. The product ratio is a diagnostic of the operative mechanism: a pure endo‑endo product indicates a singlet pathway; a statistical mixture points to a triplet pathway. Time‑resolved transient absorption (ns‑TA) at 350 nm monitors the decay of the triplet biradical; a lifetime of 2.3 µs in deoxygenated benzene matches the observed product distribution.

In cyclopropene, the high ring strain (≈27 kcal mol⁻¹) lowers the S₁ energy and accelerates intersystem crossing (ISC). Direct irradiation gives a mixture of [2+2] dimer and ring‑opened products via a vinyl‑carbene intermediate. Sensitised irradiation with thioxanthone (E_T = 71 kcal mol⁻¹) selectively populates T₁, suppressing the carbene pathway and delivering the cyclobutane dimer in 78 % yield. The reaction is an example of “strain‑controlled selectivity” where the substrate’s strain energy dictates the accessible excited‑state manifold.

Examiner traps: (1) Assuming that all [2+2] cycloadditions are forbidden thermally and allowed photochemically without considering the Woodward–Hoffmann rules for suprafacial‑antarafacial pathways in constrained geometries. (2) Confusing the role of the sensitiser: it must have a triplet energy higher than the substrate’s T₁; using a sensitiser with lower E_T leads to no reaction. (3) Overlooking that oxygen quenches triplet states (k_q ≈ 2 × 10⁹ M⁻¹ s⁻¹), so degassing is essential for triplet‑mediated reactions. (4) Misassigning the stereochemical outcome when the biradical undergoes conformational relaxation; the final stereochemistry reflects the most stable conformer at the moment of ring closure, not the initial approach geometry.

  • Common Trap: Treating the Evans oxazolidinone auxiliary as catalytic when it is stoichiometric and must be removed.
  • Common Trap: Assuming the Mukaiyama aldol always proceeds through a closed Zimmerman‑Traxler chair regardless of Lewis acid strength.
  • Common Trap: Ignoring the counter‑ion effect (Br⁻ vs Cl⁻) in cinchona‑derived phase‑transfer catalysis.
  • Common Trap: Overlooking the need for a HAT catalyst with appropriate bond‑dissociation energy matching in dual photoredox/nickel C–H functionalisation.
  • Common Trap: Applying constant‑current electrolysis without checking cyclic voltammetry for overlapping redox events.
  • Common Trap: Forgetting to degas the reaction mixture for triplet‑sensitised [2+2] cycloadditions, leading to suppressed yields.
  • Common Trap: Misassigning stereochemistry in photochemical cycloadditions by assuming the initial approach geometry is retained in the product.

Buchwald–Hartwig Amination: Ligand Design, Oxidative Addition, and Reductive Elimination

The Buchwald–Hartwig amination couples aryl (pseudo)halides with amines to forge C–N bonds under palladium catalysis. The catalytic cycle proceeds through three fundamental steps: oxidative addition of the aryl halide to a Pd(0)Ln complex, ligand exchange or transmetalation with the amine (often assisted by a base such as NaOtBu), and reductive elimination to release the arylamine and regenerate Pd(0). Ligand design governs each step. Bulky, electron‑rich biaryl phosphines (e.g., Xantphos, BrettPhos, RuPhos) accelerate oxidative addition by stabilising the Pd(0) centre and lowering the activation barrier for C–X cleavage. Their wide bite angles promote a cis‑arrangement of the aryl and amido ligands, which is a prerequisite for rapid reductive elimination. In contrast, less hindered ligands such as PPh3 often lead to catalyst decomposition via β‑hydride elimination from the amido intermediate or formation of inactive Pd‑black. The base plays a dual role: it deprotonates the amine to generate the nucleophilic amido species and scavenges the HX by‑product. For challenging substrates — heteroaryl chlorides, sterically hindered anilines — the combination of a highly electron‑donating phosphine (e.g., tBuBrettPhos) with a non‑nucleophilic base (NaOtBu) and a polar aprotic solvent (toluene, dioxane) delivers turnover numbers >10 000. Kinetic studies reveal that oxidative addition is typically rate‑determining for aryl chlorides, whereas reductive elimination becomes rate‑limiting for electron‑deficient aryl bromides when bulky ligands are employed. Isotopic labelling with 15N‑amines confirms that the N–Pd bond forms before C–N bond formation, ruling out a concerted pathway. Practical considerations include the use of precatalysts such as Pd2(dba)3·CHCl3 or Pd(OAc)2 with in situ ligand addition, and the avoidance of oxygen which oxidises Pd(0) to inactive Pd(II) species.

Catalytic Asymmetric Allylic Alkylation: Tsuji–Trost Reaction and Chiral Ligand Control

The Tsuji–Trost allylic alkylation employs a Pd(0) catalyst to convert allylic esters or carbonates into π‑allyl palladium complexes, which are then attacked by soft nucleophiles (malonates, enolates, amines) to give chiral allylated products. Enantioselectivity originates from the chiral ligand’s ability to differentiate the two enantiotopic faces of the π‑allyl intermediate. Widely used ligand families include PHOX (phosphino‑oxazoline), Trost’s bis‑phosphine ligands (e.g., (R,R)-DACH‑phenyl‑Tropos), and chiral N‑heterocyclic carbenes. The catalytic cycle begins with oxidative addition of the allylic electrophile to Pd(0)L* to give a cationic π‑allyl Pd(II) complex. The geometry of this complex — syn or anti — depends on the leaving group and ligand sterics. Nucleophilic attack occurs preferentially at the less substituted terminus (regioselectivity) and from the face opposite the bulky ligand quadrant (enantioselectivity). For substrates bearing a substituent at the internal allylic position, the “memory effect” can lead to retention of configuration if the π‑allyl intermediate does not equilibrate. Kinetic resolution is possible when a racemic allylic electrophile reacts faster at one enantiomer due to differential π‑allyl formation rates. Representative conditions: Pd2(dba)3 (1 mol %), (R,R)-PHOX (2.2 mol %), Cs2CO3 (2 eq), THF, 0 °C to rt, delivering >95 % ee for a range of β‑ketoesters. Computational studies (DFT, M06‑L/def2‑TZVP) show that the enantiodetermining transition state is stabilised by a CH‑π interaction between the ligand’s aryl group and the nucleophile’s enolate moiety. The reaction tolerates a broad array of nucleophiles, including nitroalkanes, β‑dicarbonyls, and even water (for allylic alcohols). Side reactions such as β‑hydride elimination from the π‑allyl complex are suppressed by using non‑coordinating anions (PF6−, BArF−) and low temperatures.

Worked Example: Predicting Enantioselectivity in a Tsuji–Trost Reaction
A substrate, (E)-1,3-diphenyl-3-acetoxyprop-1-ene, is subjected to Pd(0)/(R,R)-PHOX catalysis with dimethyl malonate as nucleophile. Predict the major enantiomer of the product and rationalise the outcome based on the ligand’s quadrant model.
The (E)-allylic acetate forms a π‑allyl Pd(II) complex where the phenyl substituents occupy the terminal positions. The (R,R)-PHOX ligand presents a large aryl group in the upper‑right quadrant and a smaller oxazoline ring in the lower‑left. Nucleophilic attack by the malonate enolate occurs from the less hindered face, i.e., the lower‑left quadrant, delivering the new C–C bond at the less substituted terminus (C‑1). This trajectory generates the (S)-configured product at the newly created stereocentre. The major product is therefore (S)-1,3-diphenyl-3-(dimethyl malonyl)prop-1-ene with >95 % ee.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the absolute configuration of a Tsuji–Trost product, draw the π‑allyl intermediate with the chiral ligand’s quadrants labelled. Identify the open quadrant and trace the nucleophile’s approach; the configuration follows directly. Memorise the quadrant maps for PHOX, Trost bis‑phosphine, and NHC ligands — they appear repeatedly in advanced papers.

Radical Polarity Matching and Its Role in Nickel/Photoredox Cross‑Coupling

Nickel/photoredox dual catalysis merges visible‑light photoredox generation of radicals with nickel’s ability to undergo multiple oxidation states (Ni(0), Ni(I), Ni(II), Ni(III)). A central concept is radical polarity matching: the electronic nature of the radical (nucleophilic vs electrophilic) must complement the oxidation state and ligand environment of the nickel centre to enable efficient capture and subsequent bond formation. Electron‑rich (nucleophilic) radicals such as α‑aminoalkyl or alkyl radicals derived from redox‑active esters (RAEs) add readily to Ni(II)–aryl complexes, forming Ni(III) intermediates that undergo rapid reductive elimination. Conversely, electron‑deficient (electrophilic) radicals like acyl or perfluoroalkyl radicals prefer reaction with Ni(0) or Ni(I) species, generating Ni(I)–radical adducts that can be oxidised to Ni(II) for cross‑coupling. The photoredox catalyst (typically Ir(ppy)3 or organic dyes such as 4CzIPN) is chosen so that its excited‑state reduction potential matches the substrate’s oxidation or reduction potential. For example, oxidative quenching of *Ir(III) by a RAE (Ered ≈ –1.2 V vs SCE) generates the alkyl radical and Ir(IV), which is then reduced by a sacrificial amine. The nickel catalyst, often a Ni(II) precatalyst (NiCl2·glyme) with a bidentate ligand (dtbbpy, phenanthroline), cycles between Ni(0) and Ni(II) via single‑electron steps mediated by the photoredox cycle. Kinetic studies using time‑resolved spectroscopy show that radical capture by Ni(II)–aryl occurs on the microsecond timescale, while reductive elimination from Ni(III) is sub‑nanosecond. Mismatched polarity leads to radical dimerisation or hydrogen‑atom transfer side reactions, lowering yields. Ligand tuning (e.g., electron‑deficient bipyridines) can shift the nickel redox potentials to better align with a given radical’s polarity, expanding the substrate scope to include challenging heteroaryl couplings.

Mechanistic Elucidation of Organocatalytic Cascade Reactions: In Situ Spectroscopy and Kinetic Modeling

Organocatalytic cascades — sequences of Michael, aldol, Mannich, or cyclisation steps promoted by a single chiral catalyst — are powerful for rapid molecular complexity generation. Mechanistic understanding relies on correlating in situ spectroscopic data (ReactIR, ReactRaman, NMR flow) with kinetic models. A typical cascade employs a chiral secondary amine (e.g., MacMillan’s imidazolidinone) to form an enamine from an aldehyde, which undergoes a Michael addition to a nitroalkene. The resulting nitro‑enamine intermediate can tautomerise to an iminium ion, triggering an intramolecular aldol cyclisation. ReactIR monitoring of the carbonyl stretch (∼1700 cm−1) and nitro stretch (∼1550 cm−1) reveals the temporal order: enamine formation (fast, <1 min), Michael addition (rate‑determining, 10–30 min), and cyclisation (fast, <5 min). Global fitting of concentration‑time profiles to a system of differential equations yields individual rate constants (k1–k4) and confirms that the Michael step is turnover‑limiting. Isotopic labelling (α‑deuterated aldehyde) shows a primary kinetic isotope effect (kH/kD ≈ 2.3) on the Michael addition, consistent with C–C bond formation in the rate‑determining transition state. Computational modelling (DFT, ωB97X‑D/def2‑SVP) of the enamine–nitroalkene approach reproduces the observed enantioselectivity (ΔΔG‡ ≈ 2.1 kcal mol−1) and identifies a hydrogen‑bonding network between the catalyst’s carboxylic acid moiety and the nitro group as the key stereocontrol element. The combination of experimental kinetics and theory enables rational catalyst modification: replacing the acid with a bulkier sulfonamide increases the ΔΔG‡ to 3.0 kcal mol−1, raising ee from 92 % to 98 % without sacrificing rate.

Electrochemical Oxidative C–C Coupling: Anodic C–H Activation and Mediated Pathways

Anodic oxidation provides a mild, reagent‑free method to generate radical cations from electron‑rich arenes, heteroarenes, and enamides, enabling direct C–C bond formation. In a typical undivided cell, a constant current (5–10 mA cm−2) is passed through a solution of the substrate (0.1 M) in MeCN with 0.1 M nBu4NBF4 as supporting electrolyte, using a graphite felt anode and a platinum cathode. The substrate’s oxidation potential (Epa) dictates the required cell voltage; for N‑phenylpyrrole (Epa ≈ +1.2 V vs Ag/AgCl) the reaction proceeds at 2.5 V cell potential. The initially formed radical cation undergoes deprotonation at the most acidic C–H position (often the 2‑position of pyrrole) to give a neutral radical, which dimerises or couples with a nucleophilic partner (e.g., an enolate). Mediated electrocatalysis employs a redox mediator (e.g., TEMPO, 2,2,6,6‑tetramethylpiperidine‑1‑oxyl, E1/2 ≈ +0.7 V) that is oxidised at the anode and then chemically oxidises the substrate at a lower potential, improving selectivity and reducing over‑oxidation. For cross‑coupling of two different arenes, a “catalytic” amount of mediator (10 mol %) enables selective oxidation of the more electron‑rich partner, while the second partner acts as a nucleophile toward the generated radical cation. Cyclic voltammetry (CV) is essential for diagnosing mechanism: a reversible wave for the mediator, followed by an irreversible catalytic wave in the presence of substrate, confirms an EC′ mechanism.
