Key factors influencing enantioselectivity include: (i) the electronic nature of the diazo substrate (electron‑withdrawing esters increase carbene electrophilicity and improve selectivity); (ii) the substitution pattern of the alkene (terminal alkenes give higher ee than internal alkenes); (iii) the temperature (lower temperatures generally increase ee by reducing the energy gap between competing transition states); (iv) the presence of additives such as Lewis bases (e.g., pyridine) that can coordinate to the metal and modulate the carbene reactivity. Kinetic isotope effect studies using deuterated alkenes show a negligible KIE (k_H/k_D ≈ 1.0), consistent with a concerted cycloaddition where C–C bond formation is not rate‑determining. DFT calculations (B3LYP‑D3/def2‑TZVP) on Rh₂(S‑DOSP)₄‑catalysed cyclopropanation of styrene with ethyl diazoacetate reveal a 2.2 kcal mol⁻¹ difference between the two diastereomeric transition states, matching experimental ee values of 96 %. Competing side reactions include C–H insertion (especially with electron‑rich diazo compounds) and dimerisation of the carbene to give alkenes; these are suppressed by using a large excess of the alkene (10–20 equiv) and by maintaining low catalyst loadings (0.1–0.5 mol %). The reaction is typically performed in dichloromethane or toluene at –20 °C to 0 °C under an inert atmosphere. After completion, the catalyst can be removed by filtration through a short silica plug, and the cyclopropane product isolated in high yield and enantiopurity.

  • Assuming that a planar radical generated in a photoredox cycle retains the configuration of a chiral precursor; in reality the radical racemises unless captured inside a chiral metal coordination sphere.
  • Neglecting the influence of counterions on the rate‑determining step; changing from chloride to triflate can switch the turnover‑limiting step from oxidative addition to transmetalation.
  • Applying the ionic Baldwin rules to radical cyclisations; radical 5‑endo‑trig and 6‑endo‑trig pathways are often allowed and can be favoured over the ionic counterparts.
  • Overlooking the requirement for kinetic matching in dual catalysis; mismatched turnover frequencies lead to intermediate accumulation and side reactions such as dimerisation or over‑oxidation.
  • Treating the η¹‑η³‑η¹ interconversion of π‑allyl‑Pd complexes as static; rapid interconversion enables dynamic kinetic asymmetric transformation even with racemic starting materials.
  • Ignoring the reversibility of thiyl radical addition to alkenes; unlike stannyl radicals, thiyl radicals add reversibly, allowing thermodynamic control of the product distribution.
  • Assuming that a chiral directing group alone guarantees high enantioselectivity; the metal‑ligand geometry, the nature of the C–H cleavage mechanism (CMD vs electrophilic), and the reaction temperature all critically affect the outcome.

These advanced mechanistic concepts are essential for solving the most challenging problems in modern organic chemistry examinations.

Catalytic Asymmetric Allylic Alkylation: Tsuji–Trost Reaction with Chiral Ligands

The Tsuji–Trost reaction — palladium-catalysed allylic substitution — remains a cornerstone of C–C and C–heteroatom bond formation. In its asymmetric variant, a chiral ligand on palladium differentiates the two enantiotopic faces of the π-allyl-Pd intermediate, delivering products with high enantiomeric excess. The catalytic cycle begins with oxidative addition of an allylic electrophile (acetate, carbonate, phosphate, or halide) to Pd(0)L* to generate a cationic π-allyl-Pd(II) complex. This intermediate exists as a rapidly interconverting pair of syn/anti isomers (η¹-η³-η¹ fluxionality) whose relative populations depend on the ligand bite angle and the substitution pattern of the allyl moiety. Nucleophilic attack occurs at the less hindered terminus of the allyl fragment; the chiral ligand creates a steric and electronic bias that favours approach from one face. Phosphoramidite ligands (e.g., (R)-BINAP-derived MonoPhos, (S)-SegPhos) and P,N-ligands (PHOX, Pyridine-oxazoline) are the most successful classes. For soft nucleophiles (malonates, enolates, β-ketoesters), the reaction proceeds via an outer-sphere SN2'-type pathway with inversion at the allylic carbon; hard nucleophiles (amines, alcohols) may follow an inner-sphere pathway involving ligand exchange. Regioselectivity (branched vs linear product) is controlled by ligand electronics: electron-rich, bulky phosphines favour branched products by stabilising the more substituted π-allyl terminus. A landmark example is the Trost ligand (a C2-symmetric diphosphine with a rigid bicyclic backbone) which delivers >99 % ee in the alkylation of 1,3-diphenyl-3-acetoxyprop-1-ene with dimethyl malonate. Kinetic studies reveal that the rate-determining step shifts from oxidative addition (for poor leaving groups) to nucleophilic attack (for good leaving groups) as the electrophile reactivity increases. This shift has practical consequences: with carbonate leaving groups, catalyst loading can be reduced to 0.1 mol % because oxidative addition is fast; with chlorides, higher loadings (1–2 mol %) are needed. Additives such as LiCl or tetrabutylammonium chloride accelerate the reaction by facilitating halide dissociation from the π-allyl-Pd-Cl precursor. The reaction tolerates a wide range of functional groups (esters, ketones, nitriles, protected alcohols) but is sensitive to free thiols and phosphines, which poison the catalyst. Recent developments include the use of Pd/NHC (N-heterocyclic carbene) complexes for challenging substrates such as allylic fluorides and the merger with photoredox catalysis to generate allyl radicals that enter the Pd cycle via oxidative addition.

Iridium-Catalyzed C–H Borylation: Regioselectivity and Ligand Control

Iridium-catalysed borylation of arenes and heteroarenes using bis(pinacolato)diboron (B2pin2) has transformed late-stage functionalisation by delivering arylboronates directly from C–H bonds without directing groups. The active catalyst is generated in situ from [Ir(OMe)(cod)]2 (cod = 1,5-cyclooctadiene) and a bipyridine-type ligand (dtbpy = 4,4'-di-tert-butyl-2,2'-bipyridine, or more electron-rich variants such as 3,4,7,8-tetramethyl-1,10-phenanthroline). The catalytic cycle involves oxidative addition of B–B to Ir(I) to give Ir(III)(Bpin)2, followed by C–H oxidative addition (or concerted metalation-deprotonation, CMD) to form an aryl-Ir(III)(Bpin)2(H) intermediate, and reductive elimination of the aryl-Bpin product. Regioselectivity is governed primarily by sterics: borylation occurs at the least hindered C–H bond. For monosubstituted benzenes, the order of reactivity is meta > para > ortho, with meta/para ratios typically 3:1 to 5:1 for dtbpy. Electron-donating groups (OMe, Me) accelerate the reaction but do not override steric control; electron-withdrawing groups (CF3, CN) slow the reaction and can shift selectivity toward the position ortho to the substituent due to enhanced acidity. For heteroarenes, the inherent electronic bias (e.g., C2 of thiophene, C3 of furan) competes with sterics; ligands with larger bite angles (phenanthrolines) enhance steric discrimination. The reaction is typically run at 80 °C in hydrocarbon solvents (hexane, heptane) with 1–5 mol % Ir and 1.2–2 equiv B2pin2. A key practical detail: the reaction produces H2 gas (from reductive elimination of Ir-H-Bpin), so a vented septum is required. Catalyst deactivation occurs via formation of Ir(I)(η6-arene) complexes; this is mitigated by using a large excess of substrate (10–20 equiv) or by adding a sacrificial arene such as mesitylene. For directed borylation (ortho to a coordinating group), chelating ligands such as 2,2'-bipyridine-4,4'-dicarboxylate enable Ir to bind the directing group and deliver ortho-selectivity. Recent advances include enantioselective borylation of prochiral arenes using chiral bipyridine ligands (e.g., (R)-BINAP-derived bipyridines) and the merger with Ni-catalysed cross-coupling for one-pot borylation/Suzuki sequences. The reaction is scalable: 100 g scale borylation of 3-methylanisole proceeds in 92 % yield with 0.5 mol % Ir, and the product is used directly in Suzuki coupling without purification.

Mechanistic Discrimination: CMD vs Oxidative Addition in Ir Borylation
A student proposes that the C–H cleavage step in Ir-catalysed borylation of benzene proceeds via oxidative addition because Ir(I) goes to Ir(III). Using kinetic isotope effect (KIE) data and the effect of added base, evaluate this proposal and state the more likely mechanism.
The proposal is inconsistent with experimental evidence. (1) Intermolecular KIE (kH/kD) for benzene vs benzene-d6 is ~1.5–2.0, far below the value of 5–7 expected for a rate-determining oxidative addition involving substantial C–H bond breaking in the transition state. (2) The reaction rate is zero-order in arene concentration at high [arene], indicating that C–H cleavage is not turnover-limiting; instead, oxidative addition of B2pin2 or a preceding ligand dissociation is rate-determining. (3) Added base (e.g., KOAc) accelerates the reaction, consistent with a CMD pathway where the base assists deprotonation via a six-membered transition state (Ir–O–C–H). (4) DFT studies (M06/def2-TZVP) show a lower barrier for CMD (ΔG‡ ≈ 18 kcal mol−1) than for oxidative addition (ΔG‡ ≈ 24 kcal mol−1) for the dtbpy-ligated Ir(III)(Bpin)2 complex. Therefore, the C–H cleavage occurs by concerted metalation-deprotonation, not oxidative addition. The formal oxidation state remains Ir(III) throughout; the term "oxidative addition" is a misnomer for this step.
Exam-Day Strategy: Regioselectivity Prediction
For Ir-catalysed borylation questions, draw the substrate, identify all unique C–H positions, and rank them by steric accessibility (least hindered = major). Then check for electronic overrides: strong EWGs ortho/para can increase acidity enough to compete. For heteroarenes, memorise the inherent reactivity order (thiophene C2 > C3; furan C2 > C3; pyridine C3 > C4 > C2) but always apply the steric filter of the ligand. If a directing group is present (amide, ester, pyridine), assume chelation-controlled ortho-borylation unless the ligand is explicitly non-chelating (dtbpy).

Hypervalent Iodine Reagents: Oxidative Transformations and Ligand-Coupling Mechanisms

Hypervalent iodine compounds — primarily iodanes (λ3-iodanes, e.g., PhI(OAc)2 (PIDA), PhI(OH)(OTs) (Koser's reagent), [bis(trifluoroacetoxy)iodo]benzene (PIFA)) and iodine(V) species (λ5-iodanes, e.g., PhI(OCOCF3)2) — serve as mild, metal-free oxidants that operate via ligand-coupling (reductive elimination) from a 10-I-3 or 12-I-5 centre. The key mechanistic step is the association of a nucleophile (Nu−) to the iodine centre, forming a hypervalent adduct [ArI(Nu)(X)]− or [ArI(Nu)2], followed by reductive elimination (ligand coupling) to forge a new Nu–Nu or Nu–C bond and regenerate ArI. For PIDA, the acetate ligands are labile; in the presence of a nucleophile (e.g., an enolate, phenol, or alkene), ligand exchange gives [ArI(OAc)(Nu)] which undergoes reductive elimination to yield the oxidised product. The rate of ligand exchange is accelerated by Lewis acids (BF3·OEt2, TMSOTf) which coordinate to the departing acetate. In oxidative dearomatisation of phenols, the phenol adds to PIDA to give a λ3-iodane spirocyclic intermediate; reductive elimination from this intermediate forms the C–O or C–C bond of the dearomatised cyclohexadienone. The reaction is stereospecific: the configuration at the iodine centre (if chiral) is transferred to the product. For alkene difunctionalisation (e.g., aminooxygenation), the alkene attacks the iodine centre to form a cyclic iodonium ion (bridged iodane); nucleophilic attack by the external nucleophile (acetate, azide, chloride) opens the iodonium with anti stereochemistry, and subsequent ligand coupling delivers the vicinal difunctionalised product. PIFA, with its strongly electron-withdrawing trifluoroacetate ligands, is a more powerful oxidant (E0 ≈ +1.3 V vs SCE) and effects single-electron transfer (SET) to electron-rich arenes and heterocycles, generating radical cations that undergo C–C or C–heteroatom coupling. This SET pathway is operative in the oxidative cyclisation of N-aryl amides to oxindoles and in the dearomatisation of indoles. A critical practical distinction: PIDA reactions are typically run in MeCN or CH2Cl2 at 0 °C to rt; PIFA reactions often require HFIP (hexafluoroisopropanol) as solvent to stabilise the radical cation and suppress decomposition. Chiral hypervalent iodine catalysts (e.g., iodoarenes bearing a chiral binaphthyl backbone) enable enantioselective oxidative dearomatisation and α-oxygenation of carbonyl compounds via a chiral iodine(III) intermediate formed in situ from the iodoarene and an oxidant (mCPBA, Oxone). The catalytic cycle involves oxidation of ArI to ArI(OAc)2, substrate binding, and enantioselective ligand coupling; the chiral environment is provided by the binaphthyl scaffold which blocks one face of the iodine centre. Turnover numbers are modest (10–50) due to catalyst degradation via reduction to ArI and overoxidation to iodine(V) species that are unreactive.

Mechanochemistry in Organic Synthesis: Ball-Milling, Reactivity, and Green Metrics

Mechanochemistry — the use of mechanical force (ball milling, twin-screw extrusion) to drive chemical reactions — has emerged as a solvent-free or solvent-minimal alternative to solution-phase synthesis. In a planetary ball mill, reactants and milling balls (stainless steel, zirconia, agate) are sealed in a jar; the high-energy impacts generate localised heat (up to 300 °C at the collision point) and pressure (1–5 GPa), inducing molecular mixing, defect formation, and bond activation that bypass solution-phase solubility limitations. The reaction kinetics follow a distinct pattern: an induction period (particle size reduction, amorphous phase formation), a rapid conversion phase (exponential kinetics), and a plateau (equilibrium or catalyst deactivation). Key parameters are milling frequency (15–30 Hz), ball-to-powder mass ratio (10:1 to 50:1), ball diameter (3–10 mm), and liquid-assisted grinding (LAG) additive (η = μL/mg, typically 0.1–0.5 μL mg−1). LAG additives (MeOH, EtOH, DCM, ionic liquids) act as molecular lubricants, enhance diffusion, and can participate in the reaction (e.g., as nucleophiles or proton sources). Representative transformations include: (i) Suzuki–Miyaura coupling of aryl halides with boronic acids using Pd(OAc)2/PPh3 and K2CO3 (95 % yield in 30 min, E-factor < 5 vs > 50 in solution); (ii) asymmetric organocatalysis (proline-catalysed aldol) with 99 % ee, where the solid-state environment enforces a single transition-state geometry; (iii) C–H borylation of arenes with B2pin2 and Ir catalyst (quantitative conversion, no solvent); (iv) peptide bond formation via activation with T3P or DIC/Oxyma, avoiding DMF. The green metrics are compelling: atom economy is unchanged, but E-factor (mass waste/mass product) drops 10- to 100-fold because solvent is eliminated; process mass intensity (PMI) approaches the theoretical minimum. However, scale-up presents challenges: heat dissipation in large jars is inefficient, leading to thermal runaway; continuous twin-screw extruders address this by providing steady-state temperature control and residence time distribution. Analytical monitoring in situ is achieved by Raman spectroscopy through a transparent jar window or by synchrotron X-ray diffraction to track polymorphic transitions. A common misconception is that mechanochemistry is only for "grinding" solids; many reactions involve a transient liquid phase (eutectic melting) that is essential for molecular mobility. The reaction outcome can differ from solution: for example, the regioselectivity of nitration of acetanilide shifts from para (solution) to ortho (mechanochemical) due to crystal packing effects. Examiners often test the distinction between thermodynamic and kinetic control under milling: the high local energy favours kinetic products, but prolonged milling can anneal the system to the thermodynamic product.

Catalytic Asymmetric Conjugate Addition: Organocopper and Rhodium Systems

Catalytic asymmetric conjugate addition (Michael addition) of organometallic nucleophiles to α,β-unsaturated carbonyl compounds is a fundamental C–C bond-forming strategy. The two dominant catalytic platforms are copper(I) with phosphoramidite or NHC ligands and rhodium(I) with diene/diphosphine or P,N-ligands. For Cu-catalysed additions of Grignard or organozinc reagents to enones, the active catalyst is a Cu(I)L* complex that undergoes transmetalation with the nucleophile to give a Cu(I)-alkyl species. This nucleophilic cuprate adds to the β-carbon of the enone (coordinated to Cu via the carbonyl oxygen) via a six-membered cyclic transition state where the chiral ligand shields one face of the enone. The resulting Cu-enolate is protonated (or trapped with an electrophile) to release the product and regenerate the catalyst. Phosphoramidite ligands derived from BINOL (e.g., (R)-MonoPhos, (S)-BIPHEP-based) deliver >99 % ee for cyclic enones (cyclohexenone, cyclopentenone) with MeMgBr or Et2Zn. For acyclic enones, ee drops to 80–90 % due to conformational flexibility; bulky silyl-substituted phosphoramidites (e.g., (R)-DTBM-SegPhos) improve selectivity. The reaction is sensitive to the counterion: MgBr+ coordinates to the carbonyl and accelerates addition but can erode ee by promoting a non-chiral background pathway; Zn2+ gives higher ee but slower rates. Additives such as LiCl or BF3·OEt2 modulate the Lewis acidity and improve selectivity. Rh-catalysed conjugate addition of arylboronic acids (Rh/Michael) uses [Rh(OH)(cod)]2 or [RhCl(cod)]2 with a chiral diene (e.g., (R)-Ph-bod*) or a P,N-ligand (e.g., (S)-SegPhos). The mechanism involves transmetalation of the aryl group from boron to Rh(I), coordination of the enone, migratory insertion (Rh–C bond adds across the C=C), and protonolysis of the Rh-enolate. The diene ligand remains bound throughout, creating a rigid chiral pocket. This system tolerates water (boronic acids are stable) and delivers excellent ee for β-substituted enones (e.g., chalcones) where Cu systems struggle. A key mechanistic distinction: Cu systems operate via a Cu(I)/Cu(I) cycle (no oxidation state change), while Rh systems involve Rh(I)/Rh(III) if oxidative addition of the boronic acid occurs, but current evidence supports a Rh(I)/Rh(I) pathway with transmetalation via a Rh–OH intermediate. For nitroalkenes, organocatalytic asymmetric Michael additions (e.g., cinchona alkaloid-derived thioureas) dominate, but Cu/phosphoramidite systems also work with high ee. Recent developments include the use of alkylboronates (Matteson homologation products) in Rh-catalysed conjugate addition to access quaternary centres, and the merger with photoredox catalysis to generate alkyl radicals that add to enones under Cu/chiral ligand control (dual catalysis).

  • Assuming that the π-allyl-Pd intermediate in Tsuji–Trost is static; rapid syn/anti interconversion means the product ratio reflects the relative energies of the two diastereomeric transition states for nucleophilic attack, not the ground-state populations.
  • Confusing the regioselectivity of Ir-catalysed borylation (steric control, meta > para > ortho) with electrophilic aromatic substitution (electronic control, ortho/para for EDG); they are opposite for electron-donating groups.
  • Treating hypervalent iodine reagents as simple two-electron oxidants without recognising the ligand-coupling (reductive elimination) step that forms the product; the iodine centre is the bond-forming locus.
  • Overlooking the role of LAG additives in mechanochemistry as reaction participants (nucleophiles, proton sources) rather than just lubricants; the η value (μL mg−1) must be reported for reproducibility.
  • Assuming that Cu-catalysed conjugate addition proceeds via a Cu(III) intermediate; the cycle is Cu(I)/Cu(I) with transmetalation and migratory insertion, no formal oxidation state change at copper.
  • Neglecting the counterion effect in Cu-catalysed conjugate addition: MgBr+ vs Zn2+ changes both rate and enantioselectivity by altering the Lewis acidity at the carbonyl.
  • Applying solution-phase thermodynamic control arguments to mechanochemical reactions; localised high energy and non-equilibrium conditions often trap kinetic products.

Catalytic Asymmetric Allylic Alkylation: Tsuji–Trost and Beyond

The Tsuji–Trost reaction remains the cornerstone of catalytic asymmetric allylic alkylation (AAA). Palladium(0) complexes, generated in situ from Pd₂(dba)₃ or Pd(OAc)₂ with a chiral ligand, oxidatively add to allylic electrophiles (acetates, carbonates, phosphates) to form a π-allyl-Pd(II) intermediate. This η³-allyl complex is the key electrophile; its geometry and the chiral ligand environment dictate enantioselectivity. Two mechanistic pathways operate: the "outer-sphere" pathway, where a soft nucleophile (enolates, malonates, β-ketoesters) attacks the less substituted terminus of the π-allyl complex in an SN2′ fashion, and the "inner-sphere" pathway, where hard nucleophiles (organometallics) coordinate to Pd before reductive elimination. For soft nucleophiles, the stereochemical outcome is controlled by the relative energies of the two diastereomeric transition states for nucleophilic attack on the syn and anti π-allyl diastereomers, which interconvert rapidly via η¹-allyl intermediates. The Curtin–Hammett principle applies: product ratio reflects transition-state energies, not ground-state populations.

Ligand design has evolved through three generations. First-generation C₂-symmetric diphosphines (DIOP, CHIRAPHOS, BINAP) gave moderate ee for cyclic substrates but poor results for acyclic allylic electrophiles due to flexible π-allyl conformations. Second-generation P,N-ligands (PHOX, PyPHOS, QUINAP) introduced a rigid chiral pocket; the phosphorus binds strongly while the nitrogen modulates electronics and sterics. (S)-t-Bu-PHOX delivers >95 % ee for a wide range of acyclic substrates with malonate nucleophiles. Third-generation ligands include N-heterocyclic carbene (NHC)-phosphine hybrids and chiral phosphoramidites (MonoPhos, BIPHEP-based) that enable AAA of challenging substrates such as 1,3-disubstituted allylic carbonates and allylic alcohols via in situ activation. Iridium catalysts with chiral N,P-ligands (e.g., Ir–PHOX) operate via an Ir(I)/Ir(III) cycle and show complementary regioselectivity: they favor branched products from terminal allylic electrophiles, whereas Pd gives linear products. Molybdenum and tungsten carbonyl complexes with chiral ligands enable AAA of allylic alcohols without pre-activation, proceeding through η³-allyl metal carbonyl anions. Nickel catalysis with chiral bisphosphines (e.g., (R,R)-QuinoxP*) achieves AAA of unactivated allylic chlorides and fluorides, substrates inert to Pd. Copper catalysis with phosphoramidite ligands enables enantioconvergent AAA of racemic allylic electrophiles via a Cu(I)/Cu(III) cycle, where both enantiomers of the substrate converge to a single product enantiomer through rapid oxidative addition/reductive elimination equilibration.

Key examiner traps: confusing the regioselectivity of Pd (linear preference for terminal allylic electrophiles with soft nucleophiles) with Ir (branched preference); assuming the π-allyl intermediate is configurationally stable (syn/anti interconversion is rapid at room temperature); neglecting the counterion effect (Pd(OAc)₂ vs Pd₂(dba)₃ changes the anionic ligand sphere and thus enantioselectivity); overlooking the role of additives (LiCl accelerates ionization of allylic chlorides; BF₃·OEt₂ activates allylic alcohols). A classic question: "Predict the major product and absolute configuration when (E)-1,3-diphenyl-3-acetoxyprop-1-ene reacts with dimethyl malonate under Pd/(S)-t-Bu-PHOX catalysis." The (E)-geometry gives a syn π-allyl complex; nucleophilic attack occurs anti to the Pd–P bond at the less hindered terminus, delivering the (R)-product with >95 % ee.

Hypervalent Iodine Reagents: Oxidation, Ligand Coupling, and C–H Functionalization

Hypervalent iodine (HVI) reagents — iodine(III) (λ³-iodanes) and iodine(V) (λ⁵-iodanes) — have replaced harsh metal oxidants in many transformations. The archetypal λ³-iodanes are (diacetoxyiodo)benzene (PIDA), [bis(trifluoroacetoxy)iodo]benzene (PIFA), and Koser's reagent ([hydroxy(tosyloxy)iodo]benzene). Their reactivity stems from the 3-center-4-electron (3c-4e) bond in the T-shaped geometry: the iodine center bears a formal +3 oxidation state with two electronegative ligands (OAc, OTf, OH) and one aryl group. The key mechanistic step is ligand coupling (reductive elimination) from the iodine center, which forms the new bond (C–O, C–N, C–S, C–C) and reduces iodine to iodobenzene. This is not a simple two-electron oxidation; the iodine atom is the bond-forming locus. For example, in the Hofmann rearrangement of amides to amines with PIFA, the amide nitrogen attacks iodine to form an N–I bond; subsequent ligand coupling between nitrogen and the carbonyl carbon expels iodobenzene and generates an isocyanate intermediate.

Chiral HVI reagents enable asymmetric oxidation. The Denmark group developed chiral iodine(III) catalysts derived from binaphthyl or spirobiindane backbones for enantioselective α-oxygenation of carbonyl compounds (using mCPBA as terminal oxidant) and asymmetric dearomatization of phenols. The chiral environment around iodine directs the approach of the substrate to the iodine–oxygen bond. In C–H functionalization, HVI reagents serve as both oxidant and directing group surrogate. The Ritter group showed that PIFA mediates oxidative C–H amination of arenes via an iodonium intermediate: the arene attacks the iodine center, forming a Wheland-type σ-complex; deprotonation restores aromaticity and yields an N–aryl bond after ligand coupling. For aliphatic C–H bonds, the mechanism involves hydrogen atom transfer (HAT) from the substrate to an iodine-centered radical generated by homolysis of the I–O bond (e.g., from PhI(OAc)₂ under photolysis or thermal activation), followed by radical recombination. The Wirth group demonstrated electrochemical generation of λ³-iodanes in situ, avoiding stoichiometric oxidants. λ⁵-Iodanes such as 2-iodoxybenzoic acid (IBX) and Dess–Martin periodinane (DMP) are milder, selective oxidants for alcohols to carbonyls. IBX operates via a concerted ligand-exchange/elimination mechanism: the alcohol coordinates to iodine, forming an alkoxy-λ⁵-iodane; a cyclic transition state delivers the hydride to an oxo ligand, yielding the carbonyl and reduced iodine(III) species. DMP, with its acetate ligands, follows a similar pathway but with faster kinetics and less overoxidation. Recent advances include HVI-mediated oxidative cross-coupling (e.g., C–C coupling of enolates with arenes) and the use of chiral λ⁵-iodanes for asymmetric dearomative functionalization.

Mechanistic Elucidation: Ligand Coupling vs. Electrophilic Attack
A student proposes that the oxidation of a phenol to a quinone by PIDA proceeds via electrophilic aromatic substitution (EAS) at the ortho position by the iodonium ion [PhI]⁺. Critique this proposal and describe the correct mechanism using the concept of ligand coupling.
The proposal is incorrect. PIDA does not generate a free [PhI]⁺ iodonium ion in solution; the iodine center remains coordinated to two acetate ligands in a T-shaped λ³-iodane. The reaction proceeds via ligand coupling: (1) The phenol oxygen attacks the iodine center, displacing one acetate and forming an O–I bond (aryl–O–I(Ph)OAc). (2) A second phenol molecule (or the same molecule in an intramolecular fashion) attacks the ipso carbon of the iodophenyl group in a ligand-coupling step (reductive elimination), forming the C–O bond of the quinone and releasing iodobenzene. The iodine atom is the bond-forming locus throughout; no free carbocation or iodonium ion is involved. Evidence: the reaction rate is first-order in PIDA and first-order in phenol, consistent with a bimolecular ligand-coupling step; isotopic labeling shows the oxygen in the product comes from the phenol, not from acetate.
Exam-Day Strategy: HVI Mechanisms
When asked for the mechanism of an HVI-mediated oxidation, always draw the T-shaped λ³-iodane or square-pyramidal λ⁵-iodane with explicit 3c-4e bonds. Show ligand coupling (reductive elimination) as the bond-forming step, not electrophilic attack by a cationic iodine. Remember: the oxidation state of iodine decreases by 2 (I(III)→I(I) or I(V)→I(III)). For IBX/DMP oxidations of alcohols, draw the cyclic 5-membered transition state with hydride transfer to an oxo ligand. This level of detail distinguishes a pass from a distinction answer.

Mechanochemistry in Organic Synthesis: Ball Milling, Twin-Screw Extrusion, and Solvent-Free Reactivity

Mechanochemistry — chemical transformations induced by mechanical force — has moved from a curiosity to a scalable, green alternative to solution-phase synthesis. The two principal platforms are ball milling (planetary, mixer, or vibrational mills) and twin-screw extrusion (continuous flow). In ball milling, reactants and a catalytic amount of liquid additive (LAG, liquid-assisted grinding) are placed in a jar with milling balls (typically stainless steel, zirconia, or PTFE). The kinetic energy of impacting balls creates localized high-temperature, high-pressure microenvironments (hot spots) at collision points, estimated at 1000–3000 K and 1–5 GPa for milliseconds. This drives reactions that are thermodynamically unfavorable or kinetically inaccessible in solution. The LAG additive (η = μL mg⁻¹, typically 0.1–0.5) is not merely a lubricant; it acts as a reaction medium, proton source, nucleophile, or catalyst. For example, in the mechanochemical Knoevenagel condensation, η = 0.2 μL mg⁻¹ of water or ethanol enables proton transfer and product crystallization, driving equilibrium to completion. In contrast, neat grinding (η = 0) often gives lower yields due to poor mass transport.

Twin-screw extrusion (TSE) translates batch milling to continuous processing. Co-rotating intermeshing screws convey, compress, and shear the solid reactant mixture through a barrel with controlled temperature zones. The specific mechanical energy (SME, kWh kg⁻¹) and residence time distribution (RTD) replace reaction time as key parameters. TSE enables continuous synthesis of APIs (e.g., nitrofurantoin, metformin cocrystals) with inline quality monitoring (NIR, Raman). Mechanochemical reactivity often follows different selectivity rules than solution chemistry. Thermodynamic vs. kinetic control under milling: high local energy favors kinetic products, but prolonged milling can anneal the system to the thermodynamic product. For example, the mechanochemical Diels–Alder reaction of cyclopentadiene with maleic anhydride gives the kinetic endo adduct within 5 min; extended milling (30 min) shifts the ratio toward the thermodynamic exo adduct. Polymorph control is another hallmark: milling can produce metastable polymorphs inaccessible from solution (e.g., Form II of carbamazepine). Catalytic mechanochemistry uses supported catalysts (e.g., Pd/C, Cu/Al₂O₃) or molecular catalysts adsorbed on silica; the mechanical action regenerates active sites by preventing aggregation. Recent work shows that piezoelectric materials (BaTiO₃, ZnO) as milling auxiliaries generate surface charges under impact, promoting redox reactions (e.g., aryl halide reduction) without external current.

Key parameters for reproducibility: mill type, frequency (Hz), ball-to-powder mass ratio (BPR), ball size and material, jar volume, η value, and milling time. Reporting "ball milling for 30 min" is insufficient; a full specification reads: "Retsch MM400 mixer mill, 30 Hz, 10 mL stainless steel jar, 2 × 10 mm stainless steel balls, BPR = 20, η = 0.3 μL mg⁻¹ MeOH, 30 min." Examiner traps: treating LAG as inert lubricant; assuming solution-phase thermodynamic arguments apply directly (localized non-equilibrium conditions trap kinetic products); ignoring the role of triboelectric charging and piezoelectric effects in redox transformations; overlooking that milling can induce amorphization, changing reactivity fundamentally (amorphous APIs dissolve faster but may degrade). A classic question: "Why does the mechanochemical synthesis of a cocrystal between a carboxylic acid and a pyridine require η > 0, while the neat grinding of the same components fails?" Answer: The LAG provides molecular mobility for proton transfer and hydrogen-bond reorganization; neat grinding generates only localized heat without a medium for ion-pair separation and crystal nucleation.

Rearrangement Reactions: Wagner–Meerwein, Pinacol, Beckmann, Hofmann, Curtius, Schmidt, and Baeyer–Villiger

Carbocation rearrangements underpin the Wagner–Meerwein (WM) shift: a 1,2-alkyl or hydride shift to a neighboring carbocation, driven by formation of a more stable cation (tertiary > secondary > primary). In solvolysis of neopentyl tosylate, the primary carbocation rearranges via a 1,2-methyl shift to a tertiary cation, giving rearranged products. The rate acceleration from neighboring group participation (NGP) by a β-aryl or β-silyl group proceeds through a bridged non-classical cation (phenonium or silyl-stabilized). The Pinacol rearrangement converts a vicinal diol to a carbonyl compound under acid: protonation of one OH, loss of water gives a carbocation; a 1,2-alkyl shift from the adjacent carbon bearing the second OH yields a resonance-stabilized oxonium ion; deprotonation gives the ketone. Migratory aptitude: tertiary alkyl > secondary alkyl > phenyl > primary alkyl > methyl. The stereochemistry is retention at the migration origin (the migrating group moves with its bonding electrons) and inversion at the migration terminus (the carbocation center).

The Beckmann rearrangement converts oximes to amides under strong acid (PCl₅, H₂SO₄, or polyphosphoric acid). The oxime OH is activated (e.g., as a phosphate ester); loss of the leaving group generates a nitrilium ion; a 1,2-alkyl shift from the carbon anti to the leaving group yields a nitrilium ion that is trapped by water to give the amide. The stereochemical requirement is strict: the migrating group must be anti-periplanar to the departing leaving group. This allows stereochemical assignment of oxime geometry (syn/anti) from the product amide. The Hofmann rearrangement treats a primary amide with Br₂/NaOH (or NBS/NaOH, or (PhI(OAc)₂/NaOH) to give a primary amine with one fewer carbon. The mechanism: N-bromination, deprotonation to an N-bromoamide anion, base-induced loss of Br⁻ generates a nitrene (or more accurately, a concerted 1,2-shift to electron-deficient nitrogen). The alkyl group migrates to nitrogen with retention of configuration at the migration origin, forming an isocyanate; hydrolysis yields the amine and CO₂. The Curtius rearrangement uses acyl azides (from acyl chlorides + NaN₃) which, upon thermolysis or photolysis, lose N₂ to give an acyl nitrene; a 1,2-alkyl shift yields an isocyanate, trapped by alcohol or water to give urethane or amine. The Schmidt reaction treats a carboxylic acid with HN₃ in strong acid: protonation of the acid, attack by HN₃ gives a tetrahedral intermediate; loss of water yields an acyl azide which undergoes Curtius-type rearrangement. For ketones, the Schmidt reaction gives amides via migration of the more substituted alkyl group (tertiary > secondary > primary). The Baeyer–Villiger oxidation uses peracids (mCPBA, peracetic acid) to convert ketones to esters (or cyclic ketones to lactones). The Criegee intermediate (tetrahedral adduct of peracid to carbonyl) undergoes a concerted 1,2-alkyl shift to the peroxy oxygen with expulsion of carboxylate. Migratory aptitude: tertiary alkyl > secondary alkyl ~ phenyl > primary alkyl > methyl. The stereochemistry at the migration origin is retained; the reaction is stereospecific syn. Asymmetric Baeyer–Villiger uses chiral ketones or chiral peracids (e.g., Shi's fructose-derived peracid) or transition metal catalysts (e.g., Mn(salen) with NaClO) to achieve enantioselective lactonization of prochiral cyclic ketones.

Dual Catalysis: Merging Photoredox with Transition Metal, Organocatalysis, and Biocatalysis

Dual catalysis exploits the orthogonal activation modes of two catalytic cycles operating simultaneously. The most prevalent platform merges visible-light photoredox catalysis (Ir(ppy)₃, Ru(bpy)₃²⁺, or organic dyes like 4CzIPN) with transition metal catalysis (Ni, Cu, Pd, Co). In a typical Ni/photoredox C–O or C–N coupling, the photoredox catalyst (PC) absorbs blue light, reaches an excited state (*PC), and undergoes single-electron transfer (SET) with a sacrificial reductant (e.g., Hünig's base) or an oxidative quencher (e.g., an aryl halide) to generate PC•+ or PC•−. This radical ion reduces Ni(II) to Ni(I) or oxidizes Ni(0) to Ni(I), facilitating oxidative addition of an aryl halide to Ni(I) (forming Ni(III)) or transmetalation. The MacMillan and Doyle groups established that the photoredox cycle turns over the nickel catalyst without stoichiometric reductants (Zn, Mn). For C–sp³ coupling, alkyl radicals generated by SET reduction of alkyl halides (e.g., α-bromoesters) or by HAT from a thiol catalyst add to Ni(II)–aryl complexes; reductive elimination from Ni(III) yields the coupled product. The photoredox catalyst also mediates the reduction of Ni(III) to Ni(I) to close the cycle.

Photoredox/organocatalysis dual catalysis merges the radical-generating ability of PCs with enamine/iminium activation by chiral secondary amines (MacMillan, Melchiorre). In the asymmetric α-alkylation of aldehydes, the organocatalyst forms an enamine; the PC (e.g., Ir[dF(CF₃)ppy]₂(dtbbpy)PF₆) reduces an alkyl bromide to an alkyl radical; the radical adds to the β-position of the enamine (radical conjugate addition); the resulting α-amino radical is oxidized by the PC•+ to an iminium ion, which hydrolyzes to the product. The chiral amine controls facial selectivity of radical addition. Photoredox/biocatalysis dual catalysis uses enzymes for enantioselective transformations and PCs for cofactor regeneration or radical generation. The Bode and Hartwig groups demonstrated NAD(P)H regeneration via photoredox reduction of NAD+ analogues using a sacrificial electron donor (e.g., EDTA), enabling continuous enzymatic reductions. In a complementary mode, a PC generates a radical that is intercepted by an engineered enzyme (e.g., a cytochrome P450 variant) for selective C–H functionalization. The key design principle is matching redox potentials: the excited-state reduction potential of the PC (E1/2(*PC/PC•−) ≈ −1.7 V for Ir(ppy)₃; +0.77 V for PC•+/PC) must straddle the redox potentials of both catalytic cycles. The quantum yield (Φ) and turnover numbers (TON) for each catalyst must be balanced to avoid catalyst decomposition. Recent advances include triplet-triplet energy transfer (TTET) dual catalysis where a photosensitizer (e.g., 4CzIPN) transfers energy to a transition metal complex (e.g., Ni(II)–bipyridine) to access a reactive triplet state, and dual photoredox/electrochemical catalysis where the electrode replaces the sacrificial reductant.

  • Assuming the π-allyl-Pd intermediate in Tsuji–Trost is static; rapid syn/anti interconversion means the product ratio reflects the relative energies of the two diastereomeric transition states for nucleophilic attack, not the ground-state populations.
  • Confusing the regioselectivity of Ir-catalysed borylation (steric control, meta > para > ortho) with electrophilic aromatic substitution (electronic control, ortho/para for EDG); they are opposite for electron-donating groups.
  • Treating hypervalent iodine reagents as simple two-electron oxidants without recognising the ligand-coupling (reductive elimination) step that forms the product; the iodine centre is the bond-forming locus.
  • Overlooking the role of LAG additives in mechanochemistry as reaction participants (nucleophiles, proton sources) rather than just lubricants; the η value (μL mg⁻¹) must be reported for reproducibility.
  • Assuming that Cu-catalysed conjugate addition proceeds via a Cu(III) intermediate; the cycle is Cu(I)/Cu(I) with transmetalation and migratory insertion, no formal oxidation state change at copper.
  • Neglecting the counterion effect in Cu-catalysed conjugate addition: MgBr⁺ vs Zn²⁺ changes both rate and enantioselectivity by altering the Lewis acidity at the carbonyl.
  • Applying solution-phase thermodynamic control arguments to mechanochemical reactions; localised high energy and non-equilibrium conditions often trap kinetic products.
